The first Italian to win at the circuit since Ludovico Scarfiotti in 1966, Antonelli is proving to be astonishingly good, stirring up debate as to whether he’s a potential generational great. </p><p>Managing to win after starting P19 on the grid, the youngster has managed to break the home curse and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/f1/2026/09/06/kimi-antonelli-ends-60-year-wait-for-home-winner-at-italian-gp-it-feels-like-a-dream/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/f1/2026/09/06/kimi-antonelli-ends-60-year-wait-for-home-winner-at-italian-gp-it-feels-like-a-dream/">win in front of his home fans</a>, somsomething no other driver has done this seasonuch to Italy’s delight. Seven victories from 13 races, Antonelli boasts a 66-point championship lead and is currently the strongest driver on the grid. </p><h2><b>Madrid podium prediction</b></h2><p><b>1 Kimi Antonelli:</b> Most in-form driver in the best car. </p><p><b>2 Lando Norris:</b> The fast sections and sustained lateral loads should play to the McLaren’s strengths. </p><p><b>3 George Russell:</b> Madrid ought to reward a balanced car. Getty Images]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Rudy Carezzevoli</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[From Ground Zero to Muslim Mayor: How New York changed in 25 years since 9/11]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/911-25th-anniversary-new-york/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/911-25th-anniversary-new-york/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Adla Massoud]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 09:31:26 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Twenty-five years after the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/september-11-attacks" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/september-11-attacks">September 11 attacks</a>, Lower Manhattan bears little resemblance to the place left behind that morning.</p><p>The neighbourhood is crowded again. Office workers hurry towards the subway. Tourists gather around the two dark pools marking the footprints of the Twin Towers. New buildings have filled the skyline.</p><p>For younger New Yorkers, much of what followed the attacks – cameras, concrete barriers and tighter security – has simply always been there.</p><p>But ask people who lived through September 11, 2001, about what has changed, and they rarely mention the new towers.</p><p>They talk about fear. About hearing a plane overhead. About how people treated one another in the days after the Al Qaeda attacks. They remember a city that briefly came together, but also the suspicion and anxiety that followed.</p><p>“9/11 has a lifetime impact when something like that happens,” said Alexia Crawford, an Australian-born founder of a life-coaching company in New York. “The sense of freedom has changed. You become more conscious of your safety.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DWMZZEERWNHUDCEE3K5VRA3YR4?auth=4c7c4b523b1942d668220705821bc5909eaddf6703ab420671f4dfaf4e09d056&smart=true&width=2601&height=1463" alt="Hijacked United Airlines Flight 175 from Boston crashes into the South Tower of the World Trade Centre at 9.03 am in New York City on September 11, 2001. AFP" height="1463" width="2601"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/OTW7XH3CRN2WBEKZMDHXWMXVKE.jpg?auth=4185ad0ed019ee0748a58bfa4a2179533f9ac9152e9e52506e0bf4617664e9e7&smart=true&width=3600&height=2400" alt="A man calling out asking if anyone alive trapped amid the rubble needs help after the collapse of the first tower on 9/11. AFP" height="2400" width="3600"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/NMAK6S4HTRDIJDCGSWXDFJD7TM?auth=e55ff3b1290be0b026f6fe9948dfb91634e03f4446b0a1d85fdf70a8582fb686&smart=true&width=1468&height=826" alt="Marcy Borders, known as the 9/11 Dust Lady, seeks refuge in an office building after the twin towers collapsed. AFP" height="826" width="1468"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/UZ2IN4L7XRFG3AKFH4KZNMUOE4?auth=726975a946d48f07d2decaec9ad74e4d87ff53dcfe047c1f02cbde3be92eae58&smart=true&width=1535&height=863" alt="Former US president George W Bush with firefighter Bob Beckwith and other rescue workers on September 14, 2001, at the site of the destroyed World Trade Centre. AFP" height="863" width="1535"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/M7EXQSEZNJATBDSOYPCDWIPF34?auth=ca02d7de9d676f0c07345253503f3906376b00d93ae260301a572ebda032d64c&smart=true&width=1312&height=738" alt="Businessman Edward Fine, who survived the attack after walking down 87 floors of the WTC North Tower, is covered in ash as he walks through the debris. AFP" height="738" width="1312"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CTFGQNANNCKVHEYQZDLZTKU5VA.jpg?auth=76a57e47c6a9f637d8439623e59917a8eda1968cd4d47f56bd928a1ba75cdcc1&smart=true&width=2048&height=1367" alt="Smoke billows from the twin towers of the World Trade Centre after the hijacked planes crashed into them. AFP" height="1367" width="2048"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/EVKWILZ6AYS6HLJMVA7SRKFYKY.jpg?auth=d319420f123486be084da6088934fef4ca5b06a18f0f3152f5b51a08eb462816&smart=true&width=9075&height=5546" alt="The collapse of the twin towers led to toxic dust, asbestos and glass fibres in the air, while underground fires smouldered at Ground Zero until December 2001. Reuters" height="5546" width="9075"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/6IJVCJ4V2RSJIIOFJ5DOKMXO6U.jpg?auth=80ecadf952634babd9ef0aae110d2b47374a5899aa450f616f7845adf8da8279&smart=true&width=2048&height=1510" alt="Debris covers streets including Park Row, which is nearly half a kilometre away from Ground Zero, after the towers collapsed. AFP" height="1510" width="2048"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IPID6D35JWZLBS5OCEM43UUMYA.jpg?auth=6fe5282fe96d859c32285ee42964c809df8724550f22632b9b187c0b9e2db89d&smart=true&width=4123&height=3395" alt="Workmen begin dismantling the destroyed remains of the World Trade Centre on September 17, 2001. EPA" height="3395" width="4123"/><p>Ms Crawford also remembers New Yorkers helping one another in the aftermath of the attacks, which brought out the best in the city, she said.</p><p>But the years that followed also left the country more distrustful and divided.</p><p>Almost 3,000 people were killed after two hijacked passenger jets struck the World Trade Centre, another hit the Pentagon and a fourth crashed in Pennsylvania.</p><p>September 11 also changed the course of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/">American foreign policy</a>. The US went to war in Afghanistan and later Iraq, while surveillance increased and airport security, immigration and counter-terrorism policies changed at home.</p><p>A quarter of a century later, an entire generation has grown up with those changes and has little or no memory of New York before the attacks.</p><p>Sara Bitar is part of that generation. Ms Bitar, 25, a Lebanese American, was born in New York a month before September 11. She grew up with the attacks as part of the history of the city around her rather than something she could remember.</p><p>“I learnt that New Yorkers don’t let anything get in their way of helping others and getting back on their feet,” she said, comparing the city’s resilience with that of Beirut. </p><p>“It’s something that also reminds us why American foreign policy is what it is today.”</p><p>For those who were there, the memories are different. Dulcie Leimbach lived two blocks from the World Trade Centre and watched the events of that morning unfold.</p><p>“It stays with me when the commemorations occur, then the anxieties resurface and a feeling of tremendous sadness,” Ms Leimbach said.</p><p>She has watched New York change in the 25 years since. “It is much noisier, crowded and dirtier but remains vibrant despite all the huge setbacks it endures," she said. "I find it really amazing how resilient it is. I wonder how that keeps happening, but the drive and energy of NYC never fail to amaze me.”</p><p>Jonathan Wachtel was working as a television producer when the planes struck. He was only a block or two from the World Trade Centre when the first tower collapsed and had been trying to get closer to the building.</p><p>“Two police officers guarding the entrance had threatened to arrest me if I continued trying to get into the burning building,” he recalled. “They saved my life because, thanks to them, I was just far enough away not to be crushed by the debris. They surely died.</p><p>“I was swallowed by the debris cloud. There was no sky, no air, no visibility. What I remember most was the silence. I was stumbling through darkness with my hands stretched out, hoping to find a door that would open so I could breathe.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/AV2QRD3TYJDYXHEDV6YVGRBJ64.JPG?auth=6ab3a1b3515d79dfbee1a1e128e3fb3f96753f080f4fa689dceb45446adffb23&smart=true&width=2048&height=1389" alt="The wreck of the World Trade Centre. Reuters" height="1389" width="2048"/><p>Images from September 11 have been replayed for a quarter of a century: flames against a blue sky, paper falling through Manhattan and people running from enormous clouds of dust.</p><p>What Mr Wachtel remembers most is what he could not see.</p><p>“People imagine dust. What I remember was darkness,” he said. “The cloud was so thick there was no air, no sense of direction and no sound. I couldn’t see my hand in front of my face. At that moment, my world narrowed to one thought: Find air. Find shelter. Stay alive.”</p><p>The part of Manhattan where he stood that morning has since been rebuilt. Billions of dollars in public and private investment brought new office towers, apartments, transport links, shops and cultural institutions. The 9/11 Memorial and Museum now draw millions of visitors.</p><p>The rebuilding also brought a new security landscape.</p><p>“New York became more secure after 9/11, with vehicle barriers, bollards, checkpoints, cameras and surveillance,” Mr Wachtel said. “The city had enormous determination to rebuild but the rebuilding process itself became mired in bureaucracy. For a place known for speed and urgency, it took far longer than many New Yorkers thought it should.”</p><p>For him, some of the changes were less visible.</p><p>“For years, every low-flying plane pulled me back to that day,” he said. “At the same time, I saw the city at its best. There was a sense of unity, resilience and purpose that emerged from the tragedy.”</p><p>New York has changed in other ways, too. In January, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/07/26/netanyahu-says-he-will-attend-unga-as-mamdani-says-he-is-not-welcome/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/07/26/netanyahu-says-he-will-attend-unga-as-mamdani-says-he-is-not-welcome/">Zohran Mamdani</a> became the city's first Muslim mayor, a milestone that would have been difficult to imagine in the years immediately after September 11, when Muslim New Yorkers faced increased suspicion and hostility.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/ISL4ZX6QFLJD233CDTUFMXRZJE.jpg?auth=3ba752f033c6c1fd31370330a8f8866cd66aff20b0336ddf61d398a3027c64ba&smart=true&width=5000&height=3214" alt="New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani at an unveiling ceremony for a new 9/11 Memorial Wall at One Police Plaza in New York, on September 9. AFP" height="3214" width="5000"/><p>For Mr Wachtel, the election was another measure of how much the city had changed.</p><p>“Twenty-five years later, the city is very different, and the election of a Muslim mayor reflects how much New York has changed since then,” he said.</p><p>Mr Mamdani was recently praised for releasing thousands of documents showing what city officials knew about toxic air in Lower Manhattan after the attacks.</p><p>After the initial dust cloud that followed the collapse of the World Trade Center, months of pollution and lingering dust followed, causing cancers from which thousands of New Yorkers still suffer today.</p><p>"People got sick because the leaders they trusted lied and told them they were safe to breathe in toxic air," Mr Mamdani said Tuesday.</p><p>Today, people live, work and shop around the World Trade Centre. Tourists stop at the memorial before heading elsewhere in the city. A generation of New Yorkers has grown up knowing the site only as it is now.</p><p>For people such as Mr Wachtel, the distance between then and now can feel much shorter.</p><p>A low-flying plane can still take him back to that morning.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/OGB6KE2Z6RB6RJWXJHOJBPFCWY.JPG?auth=a40ff6c3d1babd1d5294bf5e23bb341086f3473d98bb35b9c6c22ea14ef402b8&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2048&amp;height=1454" type="image/jpeg" height="1454" width="2048"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A New York City fireman calls for more rescue workers to make their way into the rubble of the World Trade Centre
on September 15, 2001.  Reuters]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">HO Old</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Ties that bind: Emirati-German love story spans two generations and four decades]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/the-ties-that-bind-us-an-emirati-german-love-story-spanning-two-generations-and-four-decades/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/the-ties-that-bind-us-an-emirati-german-love-story-spanning-two-generations-and-four-decades/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Shireena Al Nowais]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 09:13:38 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>When Khalifa Al Abri travelled to Germany as a young Emirati student nearly four decades ago, he did not expect that signing up for language lessons would change the course of his life.</p><p>In early 1987, his teacher, Karin, took him through private sessions in the German language. “The chemistry between us was very strong,” he said. He was 26; she was 27.</p><p>Now in their 60s, the couple have built a family that includes two sons, a daughter and grandchildren who embody both sides of their Emirati-German heritage.</p><p>Their story is mirrored in the next generation. Their son Saif, 33, married Anna Benz, 34, after being introduced to her at a friend's party in Germany. “It was like something from a film – love at first sight” said Mr Saif.</p><p>The family's story comes as President <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/">Sheikh Mohamed is in Germany</a> on his first state visit to the European country, drawing renewed attention to a relationship between the two nations that stretches back more than five decades.</p><p>For the Al Abri family, those ties are not measured through diplomacy or trade, but through family connections across two generations, and a life built between Germany and the UAE.</p><p>The family gathered recently at the German International School Abu Dhabi as Saif and Anna's daughter, Safiya, six, began Grade 1, with younger sister, Amira, four, looking on. Saif, Anna and their daughters arrived in Abu Dhabi in August, marking the first time the young family have lived together in the UAE.</p><p>Mr Saif grew up with both countries woven into his life. Born in the UAE, he spent most of his childhood and school years in Germany, where he also played football. German became his strongest language. </p><p>As a teenager, he spent about a year and a half in the UAE, attending Grade 11 – a time that strengthened his connection to the country. He later completed National Service. “I wanted to give something back because the country has given us a lot,” he said.</p><p>For Mr Al Abri and Ms Karin, watching their granddaughter start school is another reminder of how the UAE-Germany relationship has shaped their own family across generations.</p><p>Asked what first attracted her to Mr Al Abri, Ms Karin pointed to his openness, his commitment and his eyes. Mr Al Abri said his feelings had not changed over the decades, and if he could return to the moment they met, he would make the same decision again.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CULXVHUZPZEXPHTV3HQ5CH4UAM.jpg?auth=e7b03186f2b300acb45605f962319886cc8e770e3cf04dfb783ff37ed2c0da9b&smart=true&width=591&height=840" alt="The couple have two sons, one daughter and grandchildren" height="840" width="591"/><p>Studying in Germany left an impression on Mr Al Abri that went far beyond academics. “I am very happy I studied there,” he said. “I learnt order, discipline, respect for time and respect for other people. I learnt many things that you cannot forget.</p><p>“Germany is an organised country and a country of law,” he added. “Anyone who follows these laws will not have a problem.” </p><p>German people could seem reserved at first, he said, but that changed once a friendship had formed. “They need time to get to know you,” he said. “They are among the kindest people in the world. That is the truth. It is not a compliment.”</p><p>Mr Al Abri also felt a responsibility to represent the UAE while living abroad. “Our Sheikhs have taught us that when we are outside the UAE, we must all be ambassadors for the Emirates,” he said. For him, that meant respecting German laws and traditions while staying connected to his own identity and values.</p><p>The couple said their differing nationalities, cultures and religions never became a serious obstacle. “My family accepted her as she was and loved her from the first day,” Mr Al Abri said. “We did not face problems with our customs, traditions or religious occasions.” Rather than choosing one culture over the other, they made room for both, celebrating Ramadan and Eid alongside Easter and Christmas.</p><p>For Ms Karin, what mattered most about life in the UAE was the strength of family ties. “I love the UAE as a country, I love the Emirati people and I love the family,” she said. “Families are very connected here.” </p><p>Adjusting to the summer heat was the one lasting challenge, but the UAE became home. “There is no other country in the world where so many people can live under one roof in peace and love,” she said.</p><p>Mr Saif said his parents' marriage may have shown him that a relationship spanning two cultures could work, but he never set out to marry a German woman. “I met Anna, and it simply happened,” he said.</p><p>Despite spending his formative years in Germany, Mr Saif said his Emirati identity has always remained important to him. “I am proud of my country; I love it,” he said. “Life here is much less stressful than in Germany. That is the biggest difference.” </p><p>He returned to Germany to finish his studies, but always planned to return to the Emirates with his family. That plan has now become a reality.</p><p>Almost four decades after Mr Al Abri and Ms Karin first met, their story has come full circle. Their son has followed a similar path, meeting his German wife in the country where his parents' relationship began, before bringing his own family home to Abu Dhabi.</p><p>Relations between the UAE and Germany are often described in terms of trade and diplomacy. For the Al Abri family, those ties are personal – visible in two marriages, two languages and two sets of traditions spanning nearly 40 years.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/UPSSVJHVQBATDCD4L5HIBZUBRU.jpg?auth=d6e143e07e8badecf9bcf5a4d9fc40eae0efe56bdd3be984470374614c2a28de&amp;smart=true&amp;width=1362&amp;height=1155" type="image/jpeg" height="1155" width="1362"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Khalifa  Al Abri and his wife Karin met in Germany in 1987. (Photo by Mohammed HUWAIS / AFP)]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">MOHAMMED HUWAIS</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[As Brics turns 20, India aims to deliver a chairship of substance]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/11/brics-summit-india-uae-new-delhi-narendra-modi/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/11/brics-summit-india-uae-new-delhi-narendra-modi/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Deepak Mittal]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 09:00:19 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>On Saturday and Sunday, the leaders of the Brics countries will meet in New Delhi for the grouping’s 18th summit. It falls in the year Brics completes two decades since its founding, and in the first year that India has chaired the grouping in its enlarged form. Eleven members now sit at the table; 10 more participate as partner countries. Between them, they account for something close to half of humanity, roughly two-fifths of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/06/18/which-is-really-bigger-g7-or-brics/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/06/18/which-is-really-bigger-g7-or-brics/">global output</a> and about a quarter of world trade.</p><p>Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi framed our approach at <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/climate/2025/07/07/brazilian-president-urges-brics-nations-to-lead-on-climate-fight-ahead-of-cop30/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/climate/2025/07/07/brazilian-president-urges-brics-nations-to-lead-on-climate-fight-ahead-of-cop30/">last year’s summit</a> in Rio de Janeiro in two words: Humanity First. The chairship theme that Indian External Affairs Minister S Jaishankar launched in January – “Building for Resilience, Innovation, Co-operation and Sustainability” – follows from that.</p><p>The theme also rests on four working pillars: resilience in health, agriculture, food, energy, disaster preparedness and supply chains; innovation through startups, MSMEs, research, digital public infrastructure and artificial intelligence; co-operation through <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2024/10/23/putin-hails-rise-of-multipolar-world-order-at-brics-summit/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2024/10/23/putin-hails-rise-of-multipolar-world-order-at-brics-summit/">strengthened multilateralism</a> and reform of global governance; and sustainability through clean energy, adaptation and nature-based solutions.</p><p>The scale of the undertaking bears stating plainly.</p><p>India held more than 400 meetings across 35 ministerial and heads-of-agency tracks, supported by sherpa, working group, business and people-to-people tracks, in close to 30 cities. More than 20 ministerial and heads-of-agency meetings have been held, spanning foreign affairs, national security, justice, agriculture, energy, health, transport, labour, environment, science and technology, information and communication technology, women’s development, disaster management, trade, industry, education, culture, tourism, finance and central banking, alongside space agencies, customs and competition authorities, standards bodies, prosecution services and audit institutions.</p><p>Under the aegis of the Brics Business Forum and the Women’s Business Alliance, more than 90 business engagements have brought in MSMEs, startups and women entrepreneurs.</p><p>The outcomes are not confined to paper. This year has seen the launch of Brics AgriN, a network on agricultural inputs and genetic resources, together with centres of excellence on agro-ecology and regenerative agriculture; a digital centre of excellence for smart grids and energy storage; Brics Connect; a Brics mission for healthy lifestyle; an expert working group on traditional, complementary and integrative medicine; voluntary principles for climate-resilient urban infrastructure; co-operation in standardisation; and a logistics and supply-chain co-operation framework. The urbanisation track produced the first ministerial declaration in its history.</p><p>Two features of the year deserve particular mention.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/7TT2KEWL3NFC7PFRJ43COWSFVI.jpg?auth=857649aad5b70353036ceacbc3afe23c3db8a4be078b10a5ed3fe981992eeb15&smart=true&width=2544&height=1431" alt="World leaders at the 17th annual Brics summit in Rio de Janeiro, Sunday. AP" height="2782" width="4173"/><p>The first is the whole-of-nation method we carried over from <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/09/12/at-the-g20-summit-we-took-another-step-towards-sustainable-development-for-all/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/09/12/at-the-g20-summit-we-took-another-step-towards-sustainable-development-for-all/">our G20 presidency</a>: union ministries, state governments, industry, academia and think tanks working to a single plan. The second is the deliberate decision to host meetings across the country. Delegates have worked in Jaipur, Nagpur, Gurugram, Varanasi, Indore, Chandigarh and elsewhere; they have seen the Kashi Vishwanath corridor and Sanchi, sat through yoga sessions, met artisans, and eaten food sourced through the “One District One Product” initiative. A country of India’s diversity is not conveyed from a single conference centre.</p><p>India supported the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/08/24/brics-invites-the-uae-another-sign-that-the-multipolar-world-is-here/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/08/24/brics-invites-the-uae-another-sign-that-the-multipolar-world-is-here/">UAE’s membership</a> of Brics from the outset. We firmly believe that the Emirates strengthens the grouping and not merely enlarge it. India appreciates the UAE’s constructive participation throughout our chairship; consistently at senior and ministerial level, and across the full breadth of the agenda.</p><p>In February, the UAE joined the first meeting of sherpas and sous-sherpas in New Delhi, where the year’s priorities were settled. In May, Khalifa Shaheen Al Marar, Minister of State, represented the UAE at the foreign ministers’ meeting at Bharat Mandapam.</p><blockquote><p>We are grateful to the UAE for its part in that work, and we look forward to welcoming its delegation to Delhi</p></blockquote><p>The sectoral tracks followed the same pattern. At the energy ministers’ meeting in Gurugram in June, Sharif Al Olama, Undersecretary for Energy and Petroleum Affairs, delivered the UAE’s national statement, joined the launch of the Smart Grids and Energy Storage Centre of Excellence, and endorsed the ministerial communique. It had also contributed materially, through the energy working group, to the <i>Hydrogen Value Chains Report</i> released there. At the labour and employment ministers’ meeting, the UAE set out how AI and adaptive legislation are being used to build a labour market resilient to technological change, drawing on the experience of a workforce spanning more than 200 nationalities.</p><p>Last month, Dr Thani Al Zeyoudi, Minister of Foreign Trade, came to Jaipur for the trade ministers’ meeting and held bilateral discussions with his <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/08/31/beyond-100bn-how-india-and-the-uae-are-building-deeper-economic-ties/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/08/31/beyond-100bn-how-india-and-the-uae-are-building-deeper-economic-ties/">counterparts from India</a>. Later, Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri, Minister of Economy, led the UAE delegation to the tourism ministers’ meeting, which adopted the Jaipur Declaration.</p><p>The UAE lent its support to a Brics tourism solutions exchange platform, a practical way of circulating best practices between member states. Dr Sultan Al Neyadi, Minister of State for Youth Affairs, led the UAE delegation to the youth ministers’ meeting in Visakhapatnam, which adopted a joint statement on youth co-operation. Recently, Dr Amna Al Dahak, Minister of Climate Change and Environment, led the UAE delegation to the 12th environment ministers’ meeting.</p><p>Leaders, including Sheikh Khaled bin Mohamed, Crown Prince of Abu Dhabi, will <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/uae-sheikh-khaled-brics-new-delhi/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/uae-sheikh-khaled-brics-new-delhi/">meet in New Delhi</a> over the next couple of days and are expected to adopt the New Delhi Declaration. Twenty years on, Brics is larger, more argumentative and considerably more consequential. India’s aim this year has been to make it inclusive, action-oriented and responsive to the priorities of the world. We are grateful to the UAE for its part in that work, and we look forward to welcoming its delegation to Delhi.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/FCUDGMBJHVLTMIXN3VLDBMS2EY.jpg?auth=aada18089c2b005f704563ed2daf6f3fc1fdd7b53efbd34b90a4d99769f2ba93&amp;smart=true&amp;width=3102&amp;height=1905" type="image/jpeg" height="1905" width="3102"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A Brics billboard near the venue of the summit in New Delhi this weekend. EPA]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">RAJAT GUPTA</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Sharjah declares three days of mourning after Deputy Ruler's brother dies]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/sharjah-declares-three-days-of-mourning-after-deputy-rulers-brother-dies/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/sharjah-declares-three-days-of-mourning-after-deputy-rulers-brother-dies/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 08:44:52 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Sharjah on Friday announced the death of Sheikh Mohammed bin Salem Al Qasimi and declared three days of mourning across the emirate.</p><p>Sheikh Mohammed was the brother of Sheikh Abdullah bin Salem Al Qasimi, Deputy Ruler of Sharjah, and Sheikh Abdulrahman bin Salem Al Qasimi.</p><p>Funeral prayers will be held after Asr prayers on Friday at King Faisal Mosque in Sharjah, and Sheikh Mohammed's body will be buried at Al Jubail cemetery.</p><p>Dr Anwar Gargash, diplomatic adviser to President Sheikh Mohamed, paid tribute to his "dear childhood friend".</p><p>"A cherished friendship and fond memories bound us together at Al Ahmadiya School, just as our neighbouring homes did in Deira," he said. "Those memories shall forever remain vivid in the soul, cherished and treasured."</p><p>Offering condolences, Mr Gargash prayed for Sheikh Mohammed bin Salem's soul and the Qasimi household.</p><blockquote class="twitter-tweet"><p lang="ar" dir="rtl">نعى ديوان صاحب السمو الشيخ الدكتور سلطان بن محمد القاسمي، عضو المجلس الأعلى حاكم الشارقة، المغفور له بإذن الله تعالى الشيخ محمد بن سالم بن سلطان القاسمي، شقيق سمو الشيخ عبدالله بن سالم بن سلطان القاسمي، نائب حاكم الشارقة، والشيخ عبدالرحمن بن سالم بن سلطان القاسمي، والذي وافته… <a href="https://t.co/ro4bUpzlDb">pic.twitter.com/ro4bUpzlDb</a></p>&mdash; sharjahmedia (@sharjahmedia) <a href="https://x.com/sharjahmedia/status/2098296229121982793?ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">September 11, 2026</a></blockquote><p>In a statement announcing the death, the Diwan of Ruler <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/02/sharjah-ruler-sudanese-students-school-fee/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/02/sharjah-ruler-sudanese-students-school-fee/">Sheikh Dr Sultan bin Muhammad Al Qasimi</a> extended condolences to Sheikh Mohammed's family and the wider Al Qasimi family, and prayed for the departed.</p><p>Condolences for men will be received from Saturday for three days at the residence of Sheikh Abdullah bin Salem Al Qasimi, Deputy Ruler of Sharjah, in Al Badee. Condolences will be received from 9am until Dhuhr prayers (12.18pm), and from Asr prayers (3.46pm) until Maghrib prayers (6.30pm) in the evening.</p><p>Condolences for women will be received at Sheikh Mohammed's residence in Al Tarfaana, Sharjah.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IA2QLQOYTBHIHJ6NOJR3JNJRCQ.jpg?auth=ce6439f735f515f5c2945b119a5970fa0b3c1e986cba3460fe9653839e37e6af&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2402&amp;height=1351" type="image/jpeg" height="1351" width="2402"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Sheikh Mohammed bin Salim Al Qasimi will be laid to rest at Al Jubail cemetery. Photo: Sharjah Media]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[UAE art guide: Nine exhibitions to see, from Unseen Script at Rizk to Precarious Beings at Tashkeel]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/09/11/uae-guide-museum-gallery-exhibitions-to-see-abu-dhabi-dubai/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/09/11/uae-guide-museum-gallery-exhibitions-to-see-abu-dhabi-dubai/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Faisal Al Zaabi, William Mullally]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 08:44:03 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Galleries and institutions in Abu Dhabi, Dubai and Sharjah continue to present a range of exhibitions.</p><p>Current highlights include A Board Game Adventure at Louvre Abu Dhabi, an exhibition that traces the journey of games including chess, Mancala, Ludo and Carrom as they travelled between ancient civilisations and evolved into those still played today; Precarious Beings at Tashkeel, Hamad Al Muzaini’s first solo exhibition, which examines how people adapt to cities and the everyday behaviours that shape urban spaces; and Body Quotidian at Sharjah Art Foundation, which places recent works by Laila Majid and Inaam Zafar in dialogue.</p><p>Together, the exhibitions reflect a wide range of practices across the country, from photography and works on paper to sculpture, design, craft and painting.</p><p>Here are nine exhibitions to see across the UAE.</p><h2><b>1. In Plain Sight at Gallery Isabelle </b></h2><p>Alia Zaal’s latest solo exhibition offers an intimate exploration of the UAE landscape through paintings, works on paper and ceramic pieces that focus on fragments rather than sweeping vistas. Drawing inspiration from locations including Al Khawaneej in Dubai and the mangroves and mudflats of Abu Dhabi, the artist turns her attention to details of familiar trees and plants, creating works shaped as much by memory as by observation.</p><p>Rather than treating the landscape as a fixed subject, Zaal examines how it is perceived, remembered and reconstructed. Many of the paintings begin as smartphone photographs before being digitally altered through cropping, zooming and distortion. These interventions become part of the final work, reflecting the artist’s interest in how vision is shaped by context, technology and personal experience.</p><p>The exhibition is informed by the ideas of the 11th-century scholar Ibn Al Haytham, whose pioneering work on optics argued that sight is an active process influenced by memory and interpretation. Zaal extends these ideas through paintings that suggest the landscape is never entirely objective, but constantly transformed by the viewer’s perspective and lived experience.</p><p>Personal history also plays a role. Inspired in part by her father's difficulty in distinguishing certain colours, the exhibition approaches vision as something individual and incomplete rather than universal. Through close-up views of ghaf trees, mangroves and other familiar environments, In Plain Sight presents the Gulf landscape as a space of memory, perception and emotional connection.</p><p><i>Until September 15; Dubai</i></p><h2><b>2. In Abstracto, In Concreto: On Memory and Presence at Efie Gallery</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/HQFLLI46M5ASNI7QDICTEMTX4A.jpg?auth=bc830c04917696727c41aac9fe4fe022d1d7e3e5a004164f71bd1a9242963d44&smart=true&width=3337&height=2226" alt="The paintings prompt visitors to think about the different ways in which the human body contains history, inheritance and imagination. Photo: Efie Gallery" height="2226" width="3337"/><p>In Abstracto, In Concreto: On Memory and Presence brings together four artists of African descent whose works move between figuration and abstraction to explore identity, memory and diasporic experience.</p><p>Curated by Brice Arsene Yonkeu, the exhibition features Luke Agada, Ludovic Nkoth, Naila Opiangah and Tunji Adeniyi-Jones. Across painting and charcoal drawing, the artists use the human body as a site where personal histories, cultural inheritance and imagination intersect.</p><p>Agada’s fluid compositions draw on the idea of the “third space”, presenting identity as something hybrid and unstable. Nkoth uses ambiguous settings and obscured expressions to create scenes shaped by the viewer’s own experiences, while Opiangah reduces figures to faceless silhouettes that assemble into architectural forms. Adeniyi-Jones draws from Yoruba mythology and culture, using vivid palettes and figures that gradually emerge from fields of colour and pattern.</p><p>Together, the works challenge straightforward readings of figurative painting and consider how abstraction can reveal the complexity of bodies shaped by movement, belonging and life across cultures.</p><p><i>Until September 17; Dubai</i></p><h2><b>3. Tangerine Dreams at Ayyam Gallery</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/PO7NY6KMPRFXRJIVKDTNXNSCKA.png?auth=69e79e225aee620afa4b90a9761bef137cd0c7db575303ba070fd93312013333&smart=true&width=1101&height=822" alt="Tangerine Dreams at Ayyam Gallery explores how a single hue can evoke contrasting associations. Photo: Ayyam Gallery" height="822" width="1101"/><p>Tangerine Dreams brings together artists from different generations around the simple subject of the colour orange. The group exhibition explores how a single hue can evoke contrasting associations, moving between warmth and intensity, celebration and nostalgia.</p><p>Works by artists including Sama Alshaibi, Abdalla Al Omari, Tammam Azzam, Thaier Helal, Leila Nseir, Faisal Samra, Khaled Takreti and Kais Salman use orange as a starting point to explore memory, landscape, identity and transformation. Across painting, photography and mixed media, the colour shifts between references to sunlight, fire, fruit and changing horizons.</p><p>The exhibition is accompanied by Irish poet Roisin Kelly's <i>Oranges</i>, which uses the act of selecting fruit as a metaphor for intimacy and desire. Its inclusion extends the show's exploration of colour beyond the visual, connecting orange with touch, scent, instinct and memory.</p><p><i>Until September 18; Dubai</i></p><h2><b>4. Body Quotidian at Sharjah Art Foundation</b></h2><p>Body Quotidian brings together recent works by Laila Majid and Inaam Zafar in an exhibition exploring the human body through metaphor, material and everyday experience.</p><p>Curated by Raja’a Khalid, assistant curator at Sharjah Art Foundation, the exhibition places Majid and Zafar’s sculptures, photographs and paintings in dialogue with one another. While their practices differ, both artists approach the body indirectly, through surfaces, silhouettes, domestic spaces and the materials that suggest flesh, heat, touch and presence.</p><p>The exhibition considers how bodies are perceived, altered and contained, particularly as technology continues to reshape ideas of youth, beauty and truth. Across the works, the body often appears in partial or suggested form, moving between intimacy, play, desire and mortality. </p><p><i>Until September 20; Sharjah</i></p><h2><b>5. Vantage Point 13 at Sharjah Art Foundation</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/4BHQHBAJBRFKTP7BNM5ORHX46E.jpg?auth=5ea6dc1178be6386521ad78450125fac098b3dfb4229b2431f5899641d25b5a3&smart=true&width=6192&height=6192" alt="In her Escaramuzas series for Vantage Point 13, Brazilian photographer Luisa Dorr focuses on an equestrian team and school in Mexico that trains female horse riders. Photo: Luisa Dorr" height="6192" width="6192"/><p>Vantage Point 13 marks a new chapter for Sharjah Art Foundation's long-running photography platform as it moves into the institution's dedicated photography gallery. Selected through an international open call, six artists present newly commissioned photographic series developed with the foundation's curatorial and production support. </p><p>The exhibition features work by Adam Rouhana, Guanyu Xu, Latifa AlBokhari, Luisa Dorr, Maheder Haileselassie and Sathish Kumar. Together, their projects demonstrate the breadth of contemporary lens-based practice, reflecting the programme's evolution from a platform for UAE-based photographers into an international exhibition embracing diverse approaches to photography. </p><p>Introduced in 2013, Vantage Point has become one of the UAE's key photography initiatives. This edition also reflects the foundation's expanded commitment to supporting new work, with participating artists receiving production funding to create original projects specifically for the exhibition. </p><p><i>Until November 29; Sharjah</i></p><h2><b>6. Precarious Beings at Tashkeel</b></h2><p>Hamad Al Muzaini’s first solo exhibition examines the ways people adapt to cities and the everyday behaviours that quietly shape the places they call home. Presented at Tashkeel Nad Al Sheba, the exhibition grew from Al Muzaini’s participation in Tashkeel’s Critical Practice Programme, which supports artists through research, mentorship and the development of new work.</p><p>Trained as an architect, the Kuwaiti artist has gradually shifted his focus from designing places to questioning how and why they take shape. His practice considers the built environment through seemingly mundane details, asking what improvised interventions and habits can reveal about the people navigating urban spaces.</p><p>Working across photography, drawing and sculpture, Al Muzaini explores how negotiating, circumventing and inhabiting a city can become so routine that these behaviours eventually escape notice. The exhibition uses these observations to raise wider questions about urban planning, territoriality and whether cities adequately respond to the needs of their inhabitants.</p><p><i>Until October 11; Dubai</i></p><h2><b>7.</b><b> Unseen Script at Rizq Art Initiative</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/6B4IOLQHQ5GOLFN3IFCGHWMWTM.jpg?auth=e2e04a3937cd06a69ced0f558c37eb93d2020ce256522707a38924f39267fd07&smart=true&width=8192&height=5464" alt="Nujoom Alghanem’s paintings are part of a wider practice that also spans film, poetry, ceramics, cement, resin and digital art. Antonie Robertson / The National" height="5464" width="8192"/><p>Unseen Script brings together 10 artists to explore the routines, memories and observations that quietly shape everyday life. Curated by Meena Vari, the exhibition looks at the patterns people form through repetition, from familiar journeys and domestic objects to changing landscapes and gestures that become so ingrained they often go unnoticed.</p><p>The exhibition includes works by Najat Makki, Nujoom Alghanem, Nasir Nasrallah, Abdulla P A, Ana Escobar Saavedra, Fadi Balhawan, Ismail Al Rifai, Joanna Barakat, Neveen Abu Samra and Simrin Mehra Agarwal. Across painting, sculpture, assemblage and mixed media, the artists return to recurring subjects within their practices, revealing how personal habits and ways of seeing become a kind of visual script.</p><p>Several works draw on the relationship between people and their surroundings. Abdulla reflects on abandoned homes and the ways nature reclaims human-made spaces, while Saavedra transforms collected seashells into delicate assemblages. Elsewhere, Nasrallah draws on memories of his grandfather’s antique shop, Makki extends her abstract practice into steel, and Alghanem examines the cityscape as a contemporary horizon.</p><p>While the exhibition touches on the way technology can identify and predict human patterns, its focus remains on subjective experience, asking what memory, consciousness and observation can reveal that data cannot.</p><p><i>Until October 28; Abu Dhabi</i></p><h2><b>8.</b><b> The Sky Held It, The Earth Remembered at Ishara Art Foundation</b></h2><p>The exhibition brings together works by Shahana Rajani, Rohini Devasher and Sarker Protick to explore how land, water and the cosmos can act as archives of memory and history. Through photography, moving image and installation, the exhibition considers how ecological change and human intervention leave traces on the environment that can persist across generations. </p><p>Rajani’s three-channel film <i>Lines That World a River</i> focuses on communities displaced from the Indus Delta, where paintings and murals reconstruct villages submerged following the construction of dams and barrages. Devasher turns towards the cosmos with <i>Latent Fields</i>, monumental silk panels inspired by sunspots and other solar phenomena, while Protick’s <i>Dhaka 1217</i> documents 15 years of transformation around the neighbourhood in Dhaka where he was born and continues to live. </p><p>Drawing together perspectives from Pakistan, India and Bangladesh, the exhibition considers ecological decline across national borders, and the ways landscapes can preserve histories of displacement, destruction and resilience. </p><p><i>Until December 15; Dubai</i></p><h2><b>9. A Board Game Adventure at Louvre Abu Dhabi</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/MLC2VNGPZFDW3CSDF4PRJLTLRM.jpg?auth=feb86320473fe8c9256d36a4ea098a474f777613ab6a442c2a79fa24ef599f20&smart=true&width=5626&height=3751" alt="Interactive displays sit alongside rare artefacts in A Board Game Adventure at Louvre Abu Dhabi. Victor Besa / The National" height="3751" width="5626"/><p><a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/07/17/louvre-abu-dhabi-traces-5000-years-of-board-games-in-new-exhibition/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/07/17/louvre-abu-dhabi-traces-5000-years-of-board-games-in-new-exhibition/">A Board Game Adventure</a> transforms Louvre Abu Dhabi into a giant playground, using some of the world's oldest games to explore how people have connected across cultures for thousands of years. Designed for children but engaging for all ages, the exhibition traces the journey of games including chess, Mancala, Ludo and Carrom as they travelled between ancient civilisations and evolved into the games still played today. </p><p>The exhibition combines immersive installations with rare artefacts borrowed from international museums, including some of the earliest known board game objects from ancient Egypt, Mesopotamia, Iran and India. Highlights include centuries-old chess pieces carved from ivory and rock crystal, engraved backgammon pieces, ancient six-sided dice and one of the earliest known predecessors of Ludo.</p><p>Interactive displays encourage visitors to solve puzzles, challenge artificial intelligence, play on oversized game boards and discover how strategy, chance and storytelling have always been part of human play. Throughout the exhibition, historical figures ranging from Queen Nefertari and Achilles to samurai Hatakeyama Shigetada are reimagined through playful “player profiles”, linking games to the personalities and cultures that shaped them.</p><p>Rather than presenting games as simple entertainment, A Board Game Adventure reveals them as cultural objects that have crossed borders, adapted to societies and brought people together for millennia.</p><p><i>Until April 25, 2027</i></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/PBVMED6VTNGVLPGMUUMKUX5E44.jpg?auth=26c8e0e770ca1bbf82fc4d4a9377b88ef65ed788527312fff56c3c544fd6b93d&amp;smart=true&amp;width=8192&amp;height=5464" type="image/jpeg" height="5464" width="8192"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Artist Ismail Al Rifai's landscapes at Unseen Script draw on familiar roads and routes travelled repeatedly as part of everyday life. Antonie Robertson / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Germany partnership can 'shape future economy', says adviser to President Sheikh isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/adviser-to-uae-president-says-uae-and-germany-can-help-shape-future-economy/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 08:16:14 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Faisal Al Bannai, Adviser to the UAE President for Strategic Research and Advanced Technology Affairs, said the Emirates and Germany could work together to “shape the future economy” after the nations unveiled a €40 billion action plan to advance growing ties.</p><p>Mr Al Bannai said advanced partnerships across technology and innovation will seek to harness the strengths of both countries and “unlock broader opportunities for co-operation”.</p><p>The long-standing allies have set out ambitious plans to forge closer links across key sectors as part of President Sheikh Mohamed's historic state visit to Germany, which began on Wednesday.</p><p>It was announced on Thursday that the UAE is to invest €40 billion ($46.47 billion) in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/president-sheikh-mohamed-bin-zayed-arrives-in-germany-to-begin-state-visit/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/president-sheikh-mohamed-bin-zayed-arrives-in-germany-to-begin-state-visit/">Germany</a> in a long-term commitment that will support industry, advanced technology, artificial intelligence, digital infrastructure and energy.</p><p>Mr Al Bannai, in remarks to <i>The National</i>, said the two countries will bring together “world-class expertise” and “advanced capabilities”.</p><p>“The UAE and Germany bring together highly complementary technological capabilities, providing a strong foundation for an advanced partnership in technology and innovation,” he said.</p><p>“Germany brings world-class expertise in engineering, industry and advanced research, while the UAE continues to develop leading capabilities in artificial intelligence and autonomous systems, alongside its ability to accelerate the technological transition from research and development to practical applications, with a focus on efficiency, speed of delivery and cost-effectiveness.</p><p>“This alignment creates significant potential across sectors that will shape the future economy, including clean energy, advanced manufacturing, artificial intelligence, digital infrastructure, logistics and high-value industrial technologies. </p><p>“It also reflects a shared ambition to develop more resilient, competitive and innovation-driven economies, while advancing diversification and strengthening strategic industries.”</p><p>Mr Al Bannai said Sheikh Mohamed's state visit came at a crucial time and would serve to advance bilateral ties.</p><p>“Our technological partnership with Germany can continue to evolve beyond the exchange of knowledge and expertise to deepen joint capability development and create solutions that respond to global shifts, accelerate innovation, and generate lasting economic and developmental impact,” he said.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content
