the future of UAE-Germany relations. Photo: UAE Government]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[China’s Belt and Road expects no friction with India-led trade corridor]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/11/chinas-belt-and-road-expects-no-friction-with-india-led-trade-corridor/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/11/chinas-belt-and-road-expects-no-friction-with-india-led-trade-corridor/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Alvin R Cabral]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 08:09:12 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>China's <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/10/qatar-and-oman-pitch-middle-east-backed-financing-for-infrastructure-projects-in-global-south/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/10/qatar-and-oman-pitch-middle-east-backed-financing-for-infrastructure-projects-in-global-south/">Belt and Road Initiative</a> sees no friction with the proposed counterweight headed by India and considers the two trade corridors as more of an opportunity to work towards common goals, the commissioner of the Beijing-led programme has said.</p><p>India has opposed the BRI primarily because of the 3,000km <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/new-china-pakistan-trade-route-could-help-deepen-islamabads-ties-with-uae-1.186933" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/new-china-pakistan-trade-route-could-help-deepen-islamabads-ties-with-uae-1.186933">China-Pakistan Economic Corridor</a>, which it claims violates its sovereignty because it runs through the disputed Kashmir territory. As an alternative, it has spearheaded the India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor, or Imec.</p><p>But BRI commissioner Nicholas Ho brushed off any concerns of tension between the two trade corridors and their leading players, welcoming co-operation. China has repeatedly said it wants India, its ally in the Brics bloc, to join the BRI fold.</p><p>“There's no conflict. If you ask me, working together closely [with them] … does it contradict with the Belt and Road Initiative? It doesn't. It goes hand-in-hand,” Mr Ho told <i>The National</i> on Thursday, on the sidelines of the Belt and Road Summit in Hong Kong.</p><p>“The BRI … believe in a stable, transparent marketplace. It is an inclusive partnership that we hope more [countries] will join,” he said. “We don't necessarily need to become a zero-sum game or black and white, because different international co-operation platforms will have different memberships, and we can work with each other on these different platforms.”</p><p>Launched in 2013, the BRI has poured in nearly $1.4 trillion of investments, making it the initiative largest infrastructure endeavour by a single country, according to the latest data from the Griffith Asia Institute and the Green Finance and Development Centre.</p><p>The initiative aims to connect Asia, including the Middle East, with Africa, Europe and the South Pacific, using land and maritime corridors. It has 155 members, including the UAE and Saudi Arabia.</p><p>On the other hand, Imec, which is backed by the US and the EU, was unveiled in 2023 and aims to connect South Asia, the Middle East and Europe, with routes bypassing the Bab Al Mandeb strait and Suez Canal. It is also backed by the UAE and Saudi Arabia, as well as France, Germany and Italy.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/A5JNNRKU7FGSBCQRRVWUFDSUII.jpg?auth=146667f3006b6c807317686e3b729a089f44d7453016cf4a6a925c5bae6005f6&smart=true&width=4032&height=2710" alt="A coffee-making robot on the exhibition floor of the Belt and Road Summit in Hong Kong. Alvin R Cabral / The National" height="2710" width="4032"/><p>The Imec signatories are at the centre of a shifting Eurasian geopolitical scene, in which middle powers are “carefully adjusting their alliances and balancing” between the US, China and Russia, according to the Middle East Institute.</p><p>For India, Imec means new international data infrastructure, increased exports and efficient access to European markets. For Europe, it is about balancing in the new world order and advancing its commercial relationship with India, the Washington-based think tank said in a June analysis.</p><p>The Gulf, in turn, is looking to grow into its role as the world’s central logistics hub and develop land routes to export goods that would otherwise be subject to the risks or limitations of relying on open access to maritime chokepoints, the Middle East Institute added.</p><p>Mr Ho said the common interests both sides can work on include energy, climate change, green transformation and the digital economy.</p><p>On a broader scale, multilateral platforms such as the World Trade Organisation, the Asia-Pacific Economic Co-operation forum and Brics, as well as free trade agreements, provide varying perspectives on regional and global trade.</p><p>“But if you cut across in the middle, it all goes back to the same core, which is [a] multilateral form of co-operation based on mutual respect and rules-based multilateralism,” Mr Ho said.</p><p>“Everyone and anyone is welcome to join the BRI, if they want to,” he added, acknowledging India's “economic development blueprint” and welcoming the chance to “connect these economic engines together”.</p><p>“I'm sure we can find areas and ways, or sectors, that we can specifically work together on,” Mr Ho said.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/VTN7DLSBNRF5FKF5IELTTS7R74.jpg?auth=58b91c4f14b77397d3121262d31c0bcac85c66fe35546a1f7823f11ab4517de9&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2766&amp;height=2033" type="image/jpeg" height="2033" width="2766"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Nicholas Ho, commissioner of the China-led Belt and Road, at the initiative's summit in Hong Kong. Alvin R Cabral / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[‘Maybe I could put a downpayment on a property’: UAE players eye life-changing ILT20 auction]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2026/09/11/maybe-i-could-put-a-downpayment-on-a-property-uae-players-eye-life-changing-ilt20-auction/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2026/09/11/maybe-i-could-put-a-downpayment-on-a-property-uae-players-eye-life-changing-ilt20-auction/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Paul Radley]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 08:00:01 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>When the DP World International League T20 staged a player auction for the first time last year, no one knew what to expect.</p><p>As the powers-that-be within the six franchises tried to navigate the volatile machinations of the bidding process, aspiring recruits were tuning in from afar.</p><p>Some sat at home and watched online. Others were out training. One group of highly talented, UAE-raised players were barely paying it any attention while they had a net session between themselves in Dubai Academic City.</p><p>International stars bagged the sort of deals that are commonplace in franchise leagues around the world.</p><p>Then the name of Junaid Siddique, the UAE national team fast bowler who had once packed bags in a grocery store to make ends meet, came up.</p><p>A few minutes later, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2025/10/01/ilt20-auction-grocery-store-assistant-turned-fast-bowler-junaid-siddique-lands-170000-deal/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2025/10/01/ilt20-auction-grocery-store-assistant-turned-fast-bowler-junaid-siddique-lands-170000-deal/">his life was changed</a>, and suddenly the earning potential of UAE cricketers had shifted forever.</p><p>“I was there after training and we had the auction on in the background,” said Ali Naseer, a UAE all-rounder who played the early season of the ILT20 for the Desert Vipers.</p><p>“When Junaid’s name came up, in the first 10 seconds, no one bid for him. Then after that, there was Gulf Giants, Sharjah Warriorz, and who knows who else, going for him.</p><p>“He got to 80, 90 [thousand dollars], and I was like, 'OK, that's surely enough.' Then he went to 120, I was like, 'How is this possible?'</p><p>“Eventually, once he was signed at 170k, I thought, 'Wow, Mashallah.' That is a lot of money.”</p><p>It was not just Junaid. Mohammed Rohid, a young seamer who was new to international cricket with UAE, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2025/10/08/mohammed-rohid-celebrates-ilt20-windfall-with-match-winning-display-for-uae-in-t20-world-cup-qualifier/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2025/10/08/mohammed-rohid-celebrates-ilt20-windfall-with-match-winning-display-for-uae-in-t20-world-cup-qualifier/">had fetched $140,000 to play for MI Emirates</a>.</p><blockquote class="twitter-tweet"><p lang="en" dir="ltr">An impressive stint in 🇦🇪 colours earns seamer Junaid Siddique a well-deserved bidding war at the <a href="https://x.com/hashtag/DPWorldILT20Auction?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#DPWorldILT20Auction</a>! 🤜 🤛<a href="https://x.com/hashtag/AllInForCricket?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#AllInForCricket</a> <a href="https://x.com/hashtag/WhereTheWorldPlays?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#WhereTheWorldPlays</a> <a href="https://t.co/hcxfFzxEih">pic.twitter.com/hcxfFzxEih</a></p>&mdash; International League T20 (@ILT20Official) <a href="https://x.com/ILT20Official/status/1973382429411811482?ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">October 1, 2025</a></blockquote><p>Players who had eked out a living as cricketers in the UAE – centrally contracted national team players were on a top rate of around Dh8,000 per month – now realised this could be them in the future.</p><p>“Junaid has been playing for so long, but obviously he is a UAE player like us, so all of us can relate to him,” Naseer said.</p><p>“When a player sees that a fellow UAE player is getting paid that money, they feel like they can do that themselves as well.</p><p>“I think that's a massive motivation factor for the UAE players. You know that if you do perform well then that you can get picked up for life-changing money.”</p><p>Now the goalposts have shifted again. In previous seasons, some players had pre-existing deals with ILT20 franchises, guaranteeing them an income.</p><p>For the first time, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2026/08/11/ipl-stars-david-miller-and-heinrich-klaasen-set-for-ilt20-debuts-all-uae-players-to-be-part-of-auction/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/cricket/2026/08/11/ipl-stars-david-miller-and-heinrich-klaasen-set-for-ilt20-debuts-all-uae-players-to-be-part-of-auction/">all UAE players will be recruited</a> ahead of Season 5, which starts in November, via an auction on October 1.</p><p>Each of the six franchises are compelled to have four UAE players – who are either qualified to represent the national team or intend to in the future – in their squads. At least one of these must be under 23 years of age.</p><p>The players will get the chance to advertise their talents via the ILT20 Development Tournament, which starts in Dubai on Monday. It means 102 players will be competing for 24 places in the main event.</p><p>Naseer should be an attractive proposition for the franchises. Although he last played for the national team over 18 months ago, he remains one of the country’s brightest young prospects.</p><p>And he is still only 22, meaning he fits into the Under 23 category; with six players requiring to be signed in this, it is safe to assume bidding will be competitive.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/HFN6T2BBRJEPHBFVU6MUGNVRD4.jpg?auth=a705945bb4313f998214a1788715c3be7a1569fac37a5374299fdcb4320a5dfd&smart=true&width=4079&height=2624" alt="UAE's Mohammad Rohid was in high demand during last season's ILT20 player auction. Photo: ICC" height="2624" width="4079"/><p>With earning potential limited outside of the ILT20, Naseer took up a job with a real estate company, along with two other young UAE players in a similar position – Alishan Sharafu and Nilansh Keswani.</p><p>Happily for them, the trio have a cricket-obsessed boss at AMZ Properties who will cut them some slack if it means playing professional cricket, either at franchise or international level.</p><p>And his new role means that if he is one of the lucky players who lands a windfall at the auction, he can make an informed call on what to do with it.</p><p>“I just feel like this tournament is so good because if you can perform, it opens up a huge possibility for you to get picked for good money,” Naseer said,</p><p>“Junaid and Rohid got life-changing money. So I think if I can have a good tournament, that opens up a lot of doors.</p><p>“Hopefully a few teams will want to bid for me, and that could be really pleasing for me. Maybe I could actually put my own downpayment for a property now as an agent.”</p><p>The extreme riches of the auction have come a little late for some players. CP Rizwan was part of the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2023/06/05/west-indies-ease-past-uae-despite-sparkling-debut-for-ali-naseer-in-sharjah-odi/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2023/06/05/west-indies-ease-past-uae-despite-sparkling-debut-for-ali-naseer-in-sharjah-odi/">Gulf Giants side who won the first ILT20 title in 2023</a>. Now aged 38, he is the coach of their development team.</p><blockquote class="twitter-tweet"><p lang="en" dir="ltr">⚠️ THE SQUADS ARE IN ⚠️<br><br>Six teams. Limitless talent. One competition that will open the doors to super stardom! 💪<a href="https://x.com/hashtag/DPWorldILT20?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#DPWorldILT20</a> <a href="https://x.com/hashtag/DPWorldILT20Development?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#DPWorldILT20Development</a> <a href="https://x.com/hashtag/AllInForCricket?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#AllInForCricket</a> <a href="https://x.com/hashtag/WhereTheWorldPlays?src=hash&amp;ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">#WhereTheWorldPlays</a> <a href="https://t.co/vNRpuQltIt">pic.twitter.com/vNRpuQltIt</a></p>&mdash; International League T20 (@ILT20Official) <a href="https://x.com/ILT20Official/status/2095489364675674610?ref_src=twsrc%5Etfw">September 3, 2026</a></blockquote><p>“It's a really incredible opportunity for a UAE player to change the course of their life,” Rizwan said of the tournament.</p><p>“What else do you need as a player? It also motivates parents to invest in cricket even further so that players can take it as a career.”</p><p>In a competition where the individual rewards will unquestionably outweigh anything the team might achieve, selfishness could easily abound at the development tournament.</p><p>Rizwan accepts that is possible, but that he has been instructed by the franchise’s head coach, Simon Helmot, on the specifics of what they are looking for in recruits. And it goes beyond bare numbers.</p><p>“The instruction has been clear; Yes, talent is important for sure, but they have to be incredible team men, as well,” Rizwan said.</p><p>“That is one of the characteristics they are looking for in a player. There are two UAE players who can play in the XI [in the main ILT20 tournament], and they really have to be a good teammate.</p><p>“Even if you are not part of the squad, how are you among the group? How are you supporting each other? Those qualities are definitely important.”</p><p>An example of that might be Vriitya Aravind. He, too, has been out of the UAE side for some time now, even though he is still the country’s leading ODI run scorer.</p><p>He was part of the Vipers’ title-winning side last season, and toured Guyana with them for the Global Super League this summer.</p><p>He has clearly created a good impression: he has been retained by the Vipers for the development tournament, and will be their captain.</p><p>The sides for the competition had the option of retaining all four of their players from the last ILT20 within their development side.</p><p>It means that – at least for this tournament – the Vipers will still have Khuzaima Tanveer and Sanjay Pahal to call on. Both of those players earned contracts to play in the ILT20 in the past directly because of their exploits in the development tournament.</p><p>“It is a real opportunity to show what they have got on and off the field, and chances will be there,” Jack Luffman, the development lead for the Vipers, said of the shop window in this tournament.</p><p>“We would be delighted if it is with the Vipers, but if it’s not, then [getting a contract with any other side] is great for those young boys, too.”</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/S2ORFY7EKRGYHCJIUXNGKPRQ64.JPG?auth=e3dea3f345ced2407ea4935140a68561079d6b3f70fb621832b44f907dfe9cf1&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2704&amp;height=1931" type="image/jpeg" height="1931" width="2704"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[UAE all-rounder Ali Naseer, right, us hoping to land a lucrative contract for the ILT20. Chris Whiteoak / The National]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu"></media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[From Sharjah watercolours to a Berlin studio: Artist Nadia Kaabi-Linke on her 'beautiful UAE memories' ]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/from-sharjah-watercolours-to-a-berlin-studio-artist-nadia-kaabi-linke-on-her-beautiful-uae-memories/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/from-sharjah-watercolours-to-a-berlin-studio-artist-nadia-kaabi-linke-on-her-beautiful-uae-memories/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Tim Stickings]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 07:35:14 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>As a teenager in the UAE of the 1990s, Nadia Kaabi-Linke painted watercolours of the port of Sharjah and the skating rink of Dubai’s Hyatt Regency hotel.</p><p>Three decades on, now an acclaimed artist, she is preparing an event at her studio in Berlin where her guests – one is travelling especially from the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/uae/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/uae/">UAE</a> – will reflect on the art scene of that time. </p><p>She is speaking to <i>The National </i>on the day that <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/">President Sheikh Mohamed held talks in Berlin</a> on a state visit, and Kaabi-Linke is full of “beautiful memories” as she reflects on her own journey from the UAE to <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/germany/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/germany/">Germany</a>. </p><p>“I became an artist thanks to the UAE,” she said. Born in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/tunisia/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/tunisia/">Tunisia</a> in 1978, she first dreamt of being a dancer. But in the “very intimate” <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/uae/2023/07/10/this-1990-guide-to-dubai-and-abu-dhabi-is-a-glorious-blast-from-the-past/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/uae/2023/07/10/this-1990-guide-to-dubai-and-abu-dhabi-is-a-glorious-blast-from-the-past/">Dubai of the 1990s</a>, as she remembers it, there was no dance school to match her ambition.</p><p>Art was another story. What began with lessons from her Ukrainian mother soon became watercolours painted for her school classroom and visits to the Sharjah Art Museum, where local artists were offered residencies. </p><p>“My career started from Sharjah,” Kaabi-Linke said. “When I left I went to Tunisia and to France, but my career really kicked off in the Emirates.” </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/X274RS7ZLZFP5L2MCOSHTN4354.jpg?auth=b9d5fe8484c3d2c61db6d36a063bdd563d29a3e05b6dd8bea7790d9df35e5730&smart=true&width=4000&height=2252" alt="The flags of Germany and the UAE near the Brandenburg Gate on the day President Sheikh Mohamed held talks in Berlin. Tim Stickings / The National" height="2252" width="4000"/><h3><b>Different cultures</b></h3><p>Keeping count, that’s the UAE, Tunisia, Ukraine, France and Germany, and Kaabi-Linke’s work has been shaped by the different countries and cultures in which she has roots.</p><p>The event she is hosting at her studio, where her artworks liven up an old East German housing block, is part of that devotion to connecting cultures. She calls the event a lamma, meaning a casual gathering in Arabic. Friday’s is the third in a series that began after war erupted in the Middle East on October 7, 2023. </p><p>The war gave her a sense that “everybody is afraid to say something", she said, especially in Germany where <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2024/04/12/how-germanys-anti-israel-voices-aim-to-break-the-silence/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2024/04/12/how-germanys-anti-israel-voices-aim-to-break-the-silence/">a pro-Israel atmosphere prevailed</a>. Having just moved into the Berlin studio after another tenancy fortuitously fell through, she felt it was important “to offer my space for safe, critical conversation”. </p><p>Kaabi-Linke’s connection to the UAE did not end when she left Dubai. It hardly could have done: her father stayed behind, having established himself as a director in Emirati football. </p><p>In 2009, an exhibition at the Sharjah Biennial was “how I became known”, she recalled. Her breakthrough was an artwork called <i>Under Standing Over Views, </i>a collection of fragments that form the shape of the seven Emirates. (The UAE’s outline becomes visible when you stand beneath it – hence the play on words Under Standing). </p><p>“It was a work where I wanted to speak about the openness of the UAE,” she said. “I don't know any other country where you have so much immigration, and everybody literally feels home. There is a place for everyone.” </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/X4UEDCCGIVFADJZTVZ2NSWGPP4.jpg?auth=a75e25183c982c67deaaef8098d5d77a01164168dc60aa7b4fe94bc851169d73&smart=true&width=1024&height=683" alt="Under Standing Over Views, a 2009 artwork by Nadia Kaabi-Linke in the shape of the UAE.  Kaabi-Linke Studio" height="683" width="1024"/><p>The UAE-inspired artwork has been displayed several times in Berlin. Kaabi-Linke also has a permanent sculpture at the Dubai Expo site. Going to the UAE “always feels like coming back also home because childhood is always your home", she said. </p><p>This week she has just returned from Uzbekistan, where she exhibited a work called <i>Flying Carpets </i>that has been acquired by the Guggenheim collection. The work was inspired by a crackdown on illegal migration in Italy. </p><p>Kaabi-Linke, however, does not like to tell her audience what to make of her work. “Once the work is there, I feel it doesn't belong to me,” she said. “I want to hear the viewers in different countries with their own backgrounds, to inform me and to teach me about my work. </p><p>“Because my life is so deeply embedded in immigration and cross-culturality, I can so naturally be in a place and feel I'm part of the people and their culture.” </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/K5XO33BQ7RFJ3CNC5CXDFBIBKE.jpg?auth=0766eda3ff2e5b0ce80d353695c22ec70867e6de2ba312a73bc93fdafd343c67&smart=true&width=4000&height=2252" alt="Artist Nadia Kaabi-Linke likes to convene artists and people from different cultures at her Berlin studio.  Tim Stickings / The National" height="2252" width="4000"/><h3><b>Immigration politics</b></h3><p>Immigration is a major talking point in Berlin right now. One guest at Kaabi-Linke’s studio get-togethers was Manaf Halbouni, a Syrian-German artist who works on similar themes of migration and politics. He is one of about a million <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2025/08/27/germanys-syrian-generation-not-ready-to-pack-up-and-go-home/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2025/08/27/germanys-syrian-generation-not-ready-to-pack-up-and-go-home/">people in Germany of Syrian descent</a>. </p><p>But the mood has shifted since the “refugees welcome” days of 2015. Berlin was this week digesting an anti-Islam party’s rampant victory in a state election, possibly giving the German far right its first real taste of power since 1945. “People see the Germans as fascists again,” Kaabi-Linke fears. </p><p>Nonetheless, she is determined to keep spreading her brand of dialogue in Germany, the place her watercolours in Sharjah brought her to and which she now calls home with her husband Timo. </p><p>She compared her relationship with Berlin to an engagement that one should not call off at the first sign of difficulty.</p><p>“This is my city. I chose her. I also took the German citizenship,” she said. “I'm going to stand and resist the way I can.” </p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/JWZXZL6P35FRZPQ2BIR5SHZF3U.jpg?auth=933875bb6db5ac96bd61bc5775fec39a901d505fb09298a08da444a99e631532&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4000&amp;height=2252" type="image/jpeg" height="2252" width="4000"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Artist Nadia Kaabi-Linke at her studio in Berlin.  Tim Stickings / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Stephanie Atala is ready to tell her own story]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/magazine/2026/09/11/stephanie-atala-actress-crystal-privileges-prada-collection/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/magazine/2026/09/11/stephanie-atala-actress-crystal-privileges-prada-collection/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Nasri Atallah]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 07:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>I first met Stephanie Atala – no relation – more than a decade ago through a production company I was involved with in Beirut. It feels like the kind of apocryphal tale that gets retrofitted to someone once someone becomes successful, but I promise it is not. Even back then, I could tell she had something different. There was no mistaking that she had the makings of a star.</p><p>She was up for anything, curious about everything and, most importantly, unafraid to look silly or simply be herself. More than a decade later, with millions of fans across the Arab world and a career that has taken her from Beirut to Cairo, Istanbul, Paris and beyond, remarkably little seems to have changed.</p><p>On a breezy August day in Faitroun, Mount Lebanon, Mohamad Al-Rifai, the photographer on our <i>TN </i>cover shoot, asks the Lebanese-French actress if she’s comfortable climbing on to an edge of the cliff-face for a shot. Atala surveys the options and instead suggests an even more precarious perch that would make a better image.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IUQ43LV5G5AB7CY7QZQJEVAPGI.jpg?auth=1680f25978e85c6fbe0edaaa8c5c04c6f9a05c4833d338cc06847900f61f95e7&smart=true&width=4368&height=5824" alt="Zip-front wool cardigan; Duchess dress; woollen scarf; over-the-knee socks; and pumps, all from Prada" height="5824" width="4368"/><p>“I am worried. I am always scared,” she tells me when we speak a few days later. “I always say: ‘What if I can’t do this?’ I always doubt myself, but that doesn’t mean that it stops me from actually doing it.”</p><p>It is a contradiction that lies at the heart of Atala’s career. The apparent fearlessness has always contained plenty of fear; she has simply become good at moving towards it. “I feel like it’s this positive stress that’s always pushing me forward,” she says.</p><p>Today, Atala, 32, is in an enviable position. She has become one of the region’s most recognisable television stars, particularly after the enormous success of the 90-episode drama <i>Crystal</i>. She has made films, released music, worked across the Arab world and, most recently, crossed into French television with the HBO series <i>Privileges</i>. Yet ask whether she feels she has arrived and the answer is an immediate no.</p><p>“I’m not sure if anyone ever feels like they’ve achieved what they want to achieve in life,” she says. “I might be in a great place at the moment in my life, career-wise and on a personal level as well, but I feel like there’s a lot more to do.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5567INDULBCZJO4C4G7DFGIQRY.jpg?auth=0a17570be4b163f62511a35b8983415e2ebdaca207bd3c5453c99895eb7074a4&smart=true&width=4368&height=5824" alt="Devore poplin jacket; printed Compact dress; sheer Compact dress; embroidered over-the-knee socks; and pumps, all from Prada" height="5824" width="4368"/><p>There are, she says, “things that people don’t know I can do”. One is writing. Atala has completed a screenplay she hopes to film, although she is reluctant to reveal much about it. There is a love story, she allows. There is Beirut. There is an element of fantasy, or what sounds to me like magical realism. And there is plenty of Atala herself.</p><p>“It’s inspired by my life as well, by stuff I’ve been through, and things we’ve been through in Lebanon and in the region,” she says. “In my relationship, even, I’ve borrowed some stuff that my husband has gone through. I feel like this script is a mixing bowl of everything that I am and everything that I’d like to tell.”</p><p>Writing makes sense when Atala explains how she sees herself. Acting and music might look like parallel careers, with writing now adding a third dimension, but to her they are expressions of the same thing. “At the core of who I am, I feel like I’m a storyteller,” she says.</p><p>The funny thing is that a career devoted to storytelling began by accident. Atala was four when she found herself in a restaurant where an advertisement was being filmed. Another child was struggling with the scene, so Atala wandered over to help. Somehow, she ended up appearing in the commercial herself. As a teenager, more advertising work followed.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/W6JD24WBDZD3DHAOYXNR27I4BI.jpg?auth=f365c25041b855b9265eec251bec57a99cb663516694330bb39ef95010903237&smart=true&width=4368&height=5824" alt="Wool and cashmere sweater; gabardine midiskirt; and Bonnie leather bag, all from Prada" height="5824" width="4368"/><p>Atala nevertheless went on to study art direction at the Lebanese Academy of Fine Arts, seemingly destined for a life behind the camera. Before long, she found herself in four short films in the same film festival. That was where Lebanese actor and director Georges Khabbaz invited her to audition for his play. He cast her. That same week, Atala auditioned for a Ramadan television series and landed that, too. Suddenly she was filming television, rushing to the theatre to perform every night, then returning to the set.</p><p>“My life was just a tornado from that moment on,” she says. “From year one, I was fully booked and it was crazy.” It has, more or less, remained that way.</p><p>There is something telling about Atala’s insistence that all of this happened accidentally. Opportunity might have repeatedly presented itself, but she had to keep saying yes. Beneath that willingness was a shy young woman who, she says, perhaps wanted exactly this all along.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/GCMNDEIBHJBL7CHEUNZEJTPM2U.jpg?auth=65fd880cd856c249ede6c7f2e706a301b878986bbb62f1e78d23e7b44bdf442e&smart=true&width=4368&height=5824" alt="Wool and cashmere top; poplin top; embroidered organdy dress; embroidered organdy midiskirt; over-the-knee socks; and pumps, all from Prada" height="5824" width="4368"/><p>“I feel like I always wanted this, but I was always too shy to ask for it,” she says. “And then one day I was just ready for it.” Some of that shyness remains, although Atala says she knows herself better today. “I like myself more,” she says. “I’m proud of who I am. I’m proud that I’m a good person. I’m kind. I have a good circle of friends. I love my family. I’m there for them.”</p><p>That grounding is important, particularly because Atala has made the unusual choice to build an increasingly international career without really leaving home. Her French passport – her mother is French – means she could have relocated to Europe at any point, just as Cairo might have offered a more obvious base for a pan-Arab acting career. She never seriously wanted either. “I’ve always had the option and I never took it,” she says. “I’m choosing to live in Beirut because I deeply love it here. I cannot live anywhere else.”</p><p>Instead, the work comes and Atala goes to meet it. She spent months in Paris filming <i>Privileges</i>. Before that came two long productions in Istanbul. She is currently filming in Egypt, with another series in Beirut to follow. There have been projects in Tunisia, Morocco, Cyprus, the UAE and elsewhere.</p><p>The turning point came with <i>Crystal </i>in 2023, which transformed Atala from a successful working actress into a pan-Arab star. The series topped MBC Shahid and made her a nightly presence in homes across the region. The scale only really became apparent when she travelled and started getting recognised.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/VMXCIRC7FBA3DC6EXF2V7VB3PI.jpg?auth=a77fdd2f1a01cb6e394e574c5a4d7f21e5eace4f60bfcd802846c1f665c4b0e2&smart=true&width=5824&height=4368" alt="Poplin shirt; and Bonnie leather mini-bag, both from Prada" height="4368" width="5824"/><p>“The more exposure you have, the more pressure and responsibility you have,” she says. That kind of long-form television popular across the Middle East and North Africa creates a peculiar intimacy. Spend 90 evenings in someone’s living room and the boundary between actor and character can become surprisingly porous.</p><p>“I am called by many names,” Atala says with a laugh. “I’m called Faye, I’m called Mirna, I’m called by all the names of the characters because [the audience] feel like you’re part of their lives now. You’re in their house every night at the same time, and they feel like they know you.”</p><p>Sometimes strangers simply ask to hug her. After she played the villainous Mirna in Salma, however, encounters could be less affectionate. People would approach her demanding: “Why did you do that to your sister?” She remember responding: “Guys, I’m not her. I am an actress.”</p><p>It is partly why Atala maintains a deliberately unpolished social media presence. Between glamorous shoots and red carpets are occasional goofy videos, many originally intended for her parents or her husband before she decided to share them. “[The audience] forgets who you are as a person. It’s good to be relatable because we’re all actually the same.”</p><p>That distinction between the career and the person appears important to Atala. Acting is a huge part of her life, she says, but “it’s not everything”. “I’m not willing to lose the connection I have with my country, the connection I have with my husband, with my parents, with my friends. I feel like this is the thing I cherish most.”</p><p>Her husband, musician Zef, occupies an interesting position within both worlds. Their schedules are frequently chaotic, but their creative lives are deeply intertwined. “Art is part of our relationship,” Atala says. “We’re always discussing it, always dreaming together and aiming for more for each other.”</p><p>It was Zef who, during a particularly stressful production, offered advice that changed how Atala approaches acting. She had become anxious about everything happening around her – schedules, direction, decisions beyond her control. “Steph, stop being so anxious,” she remembers him saying. “This is not your project. It’s someone else’s responsibility. Just go and play the role they got you to play.” From that advice, she learnt to enjoy the work more. </p><p>It is an approach that has served her well. Her recent French production came almost as unexpectedly as the opportunities at the beginning of her career: an email from a casting director, a Zoom audition, a callback with the directors and then the <i>Privileges </i>role. More European auditions have followed.</p><p>For now, though, she isn’t uprooting her life to chase them. She seems content to allow some of those happy accidents to continue finding her. Atala has spent more than a decade taking the opportunities that appeared in front of her, climbing on to whichever precarious rock presented itself and seeing where it might lead. Now, increasingly, she wants to decide where the story goes.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/FMAX6AOLEVBSJHL377I6RGPAKQ.jpg?auth=7983fa51548c8177fb79ecacd7f2b3a6563b7e6424fb09c82ea9bde218dfdc97&smart=true&width=3000&height=4000" alt="September 2026 cover of The National's TN magazine featuring Stephanie Atala. Mohamad Al-Rifai for The National" height="4000" width="3000"/><p><b>Fashion director:</b> Sarah Maisey</p><p><b>Photographer: </b>Mohamad “Rifo” Al-Rifai </p><p><b>Hairstylist:</b> Moe Rida </p><p><b>Make-up artist: </b>Kevork Tavitian</p><p><b>Assistant stylist:</b> Joe Daccache </p><p><b>Assistant photographer:</b> Joe Massoud</p><p><b>Executive producer:</b> Samer Fleihan</p><p><b>Production co-ordinator: </b>Carine Malek</p><p><b>Production house: </b>Fifteen O Five</p><p><b>Location manager:</b> Pin Your Film</p><p><b>Unit Manager:</b> Ali Sbeity</p><p><b>Shot on location</b> in Faitroun, Lebanon</p><p><i><b>Photo shoot created in partnership with Prada</b></i></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/726ZMOFEPBE4HFNBZPVARGAOYE.jpg?auth=ccbf1a4621b80c381b53ea64103de669dfff2be70c80ddb859b0ea1ec82b3476&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5824&amp;height=3276" type="image/jpeg" height="3276" width="5824"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[

Photo: UAE Presidential Court]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Ryan Carter / UAE Presidential Court</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[London’s mortgage market opens up to Gulf buyers]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uk/2026/09/11/londons-mortgage-market-opens-up-to-gulf-buyers/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uk/2026/09/11/londons-mortgage-market-opens-up-to-gulf-buyers/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Paul Carey]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 06:42:18 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Dozens of UK lenders have started offering mortgages to international buyers, opening up the property market to new investors other than British citizens working overseas, analysis shared with <i>The National</i> has found.</p><p>Three in five top-tier lenders now have a route for overseas borrowers, as a growing market develops in which Gulf-based families maintain or acquire <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uk/2026/06/26/steep-property-discounts-lure-gulf-buyers-back-to-london/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uk/2026/06/26/steep-property-discounts-lure-gulf-buyers-back-to-london/">UK property</a> using British mortgage finance while keeping their principal residence, businesses and liquidity in the Middle East.</p><p>Transaction data includes a private-bank mortgage of about £8.5 million ($11.4 million) on prime London residential property, where income denominated in Qatari riyals was accepted, as well as recent mortgage terms for UAE-resident borrowers earning in dirhams. In another case, a Gulf-resident first time-buyer couple secured an agreement in principle for an 80 per cent loan-to-value UK mortgage.</p><p>The analysis by <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2023/12/08/billionaires-opting-to-rent-rather-than-buy-london-mansions/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2023/12/08/billionaires-opting-to-rent-rather-than-buy-london-mansions/">Enness Global</a> suggests the perception that Gulf buyers of London property are predominantly cash purchasers may be wide of the mark. </p><p>It shows a widening group of banks, building societies and specialist lenders will now finance UK property for borrowers whose home, employment, company or principal assets are in the Middle East.</p><p>Of 122 Tier-A lenders in its database of 1,000 UK and international lenders, 73 lend to people who are not resident in the UK.</p><p>Of those, 47 explicitly lend to non-resident foreign citizens. Enness Global identified 21 leading UK lenders with specific lending criteria or transaction evidence relating to borrowers based in the UAE, Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Kuwait, Oman or Bahrain.</p><p>Private banks, meanwhile, were offering larger loans and assessing net worth, overseas earnings and investments rather than sticking to conventional UK salary-based affordability criteria.</p><p>Islay Robinson, chief executive of the Mayfair-based broker, said: “London and Dubai are often described as competing wealth centres. Increasingly they are complementary. Dubai may be where somebody lives, runs their business and holds much of their liquidity, while London remains somewhere they want a home, an investment property, children at school or simply a second financial base.</p><p>“Ten years ago, there was an assumption that if a wealthy Middle Eastern buyer wanted a London property, they would simply pay cash. That assumption is increasingly wrong.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/QCYUCLDU6BEHHAWCR3LEDZH4XY.jpg?auth=717c0ae2a9b8cfc5a9e5ca2feffe222f51ca4e6a55762a325e22aadd8d8f8fa0&smart=true&width=8660&height=5773" alt="Enness Global analysis suggests many wealthy families who have moved from the UK to the Gulf have kept financial links in London. " height="5773" width="8660"/><h2><b>Who is the 'Dubai buyer?'</b></h2><p>He said the typical ‘Dubai buyer’ was no longer limited to Emiratis, as wealthy entrepreneurs and investors who have swapped Britain for the UAE after the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/07/31/more-than-1000-non-doms-left-uk-before-tax-reform-took-effect/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/07/31/more-than-1000-non-doms-left-uk-before-tax-reform-took-effect/">non-dom tax status was scrapped</a> may not have severed financial links with London entirely. </p><p>The diversity of lenders competing for these borrowers was also significant, he said. </p><p>“At one end of the market, specialist building societies and international mortgage lenders will finance relatively conventional London homes and investment properties for employed expatriates. At the other, private banks can combine UK mortgages with securities-backed lending, multi-currency facilities and offshore wealth structures for ultra-high-net-worth families.”</p><p>UK house prices rose in August, according to one of the country’s top mortgage lenders, suggesting demand is holding up despite the economic effects of ongoing conflict between the US and Iran.</p><h2><b>Price rises</b></h2><p>Nationwide Building Society said the average price of a home rose 0.2 per cent to £275,465 in August, reversing a downwardly revised 0.1 per cent drop the previous month. The latest month’s reading was slightly stronger than the 0.1 per cent increase expected by economists.</p><p>The reading implies Britain’s housing market may be weathering the Middle East conflict. Healthy savings buffers and a boost to sentiment from Prime Minister Andy Burnham’s cost-of-living measures are helping sustain demand, although energy costs remain high and prices have fallen in two of the last four months.</p><p>“Underlying affordability is improving, as house price growth remains well below earnings growth,” said Robert Gardner, Nationwide’s chief economist. Higher mortgage rates, however, are offsetting some of those gains, he added.</p><p>The Iran energy shock has dashed hopes of interest-rate cuts, with traders now expecting a quarter-point increase by the end of the year.</p><p>The average five-year fixed-rate mortgage remains around 0.8 percentage points higher than before the war at 5.63 per cent, according to Moneyfacts.</p><p>Official figures, which are based on completed transactions, showed house prices falling for the first time this year in June. Property website Rightmove also said asking prices fell 2 per cent in August, posting their biggest monthly drop since 2022, with particularly weak figures in London.</p><p>New Chancellor of the Exchequer John Healey will deliver his first budget next month hoping to avoid any harm to the economy from speculation about tax rises – a risk that could further discourage prospective buyers.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5IZF2C2RZNF7JHMTBNXBEWZOK4.jpg?auth=979c39c36c6c30e730f0fb9887f27fdc9428c26317083c886150360a84a0069f&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5000&amp;height=3335" type="image/jpeg" height="3335" width="5000"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Buying a London property via a mortgage has become a realistic proposition, research suggests. Returning to a part-time job is an opportunity to keep myself going,” Mr Al Marzouqi said.</p><p>“It is not about financial revenue. It makes me feel that I can help people with my experience. Interacting with people at work will enhance the mental health of senior citizens.”</p><p>Hussain Al Darmaki, 56, said there was a big turnout of applicants for the part-time jobs this week after the plan was announced.</p><p>“Many people are applying for the jobs in Sharjah government. It is a big step to safeguard senior citizens in Sharjah.</p><p>“The elderly [person] will feel alive again by going to work that he can do without a burden on his physical or health situation.”</p><p>Mr Al Darmaki, a retired businessman, said such initiatives were important to support the elderly.</p><p>“Retirement changes a lot in the human nature. Having activities or programmes like part-time working will enhance positive behaviour for senior citizens,” he said.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/TVOX54YNIBGL3GPMQU3ESB6JJE.jpg?auth=68a8476e1d34e65663214e5e84906ed40791be42eebdf675de862ec68d2d187e&smart=true&width=2918&height=1641" alt="Sheikh Dr Sultan bin Muhammad Al Qasimi also wants to ensure 3,000 UAE citizens are working in Sharjah's public sector by the end of the year. Omar Al Askar / UAE Presidential Court" height="1641" width="2918"/><h2><b>Keeping active</b></h2><p>“I want to get them outside the house and participate with us. It to prevent depression. Even if they come walking with a stick,” Sheikh Dr Al Qasimi told the Direct Line radio station this week.</p><p>“I want the elderly to participate with us in the part-time work plan. They still have something to offer.”</p><p>Sharjah's Ruler emphasised that the new system would help to lift senior citizens' spirits as well as enhancing their quality of life and their mental health.</p><p>“Ask the elderly [man] what he is waiting for each day sitting at home and the answer may be a painful one. I will bring him and his wife out of the house and work even for one or two hours per day depending on what they can manage,” Sheikh Al Qasimi added.</p><p>“They won’t find any hardship in this work because the jobs will be undemanding.”</p><p>As an example of the type of roles available under the scheme, he cited checking visitors' tickets at Sharjah’s museums.</p><p>“The senior employee will wake up early, get dressed, leave the house to work to see people and interact,” Sheikh Dr Sultan said.</p><p>Male employees in emirate's public sector can retire at the age of 55, or once they have reached 20 years of service, according to the Sharjah government website. </p><p>If an employee opts to retire before 55, they will receive only a percentage of their pension. Full payments will begin once they reach mandatory retirement age.</p><p>The same applies to female employees, except the mandatory age for them is 50. Women who have children under the age of 18 can retire at 45.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/Z366NMFZJWUOJ6CKQQDHGRJJCY.jpg?auth=8faf6035716daefcb1a90cd9a68e48b1f95616efe739c84810e22fc53991d375&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5184&amp;height=3456" type="image/jpeg" height="3456" width="5184"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A plan has been approved to bring senior citizens, their spouses and retired people back to work part-time in Sharjah's public sector. Ravindranath K / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Yemeni rebels used Anthropic's AI software to design and build guided weapons]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/how-yemeni-rebels-used-anthropics-ai-software-to-design-and-build-guided-weapons/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/how-yemeni-rebels-used-anthropics-ai-software-to-design-and-build-guided-weapons/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Mohamad Ali  Harisi]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 05:34:50 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Anthropic has investigated and disrupted “threat actors” which used its AI software for military purposes, including in Houthi-held territory in Yemen.</p><p>In the report <i>Detecting and Countering Misuse of AI</i>, the company described an ambitious weapons development effort in northern Yemen, where a group was working on <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/gulf-states-at-un-condemn-houthi-attacks-on-saudi-arabia-as-rebels-seize-key-yemen-port/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/gulf-states-at-un-condemn-houthi-attacks-on-saudi-arabia-as-rebels-seize-key-yemen-port/">three sophisticated missile projects</a> simultaneously.</p><p>Iran-backed Houthis use the capital Sanaa and northern Yemen, which they control, as a launch pad for attacks against neighbours. On Thursday, the rebels seized the strategic port city of Mokha on the western coast, further expanding their control along the Red Sea and near the Bab Al Mandeb shipping route.</p><p>The report provided astonishing detail about the group’s work, offering a rare glimpse into the scale and sophistication of its weapons ambitions.</p><p>One involved turning commercially available technology into a guided rocket, using an off-the-shelf, phone-grade flight computer and a homing system designed to steer it towards its target during the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/10/live-iran-us-war-houthis-yemen/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/10/live-iran-us-war-houthis-yemen/">final phase of flight</a>.</p><p>A second project was far more ambitious: a multistage ballistic missile that the group said it wanted to send across more than 2,000km.</p><p>The most striking, however, was called the R2000 programme, a family of proposed missile variants that included a hypersonic glide vehicle, a technology normally associated with some of the world’s most advanced military powers.</p><p>“The activity took place between December 2025 and August 2026, involved Claude Haiku, Sonnet and Opus models, and was disrupted by Anthropic, which banned the accounts and published the case in the report,” the company said.</p><p>According to Anthropic, rather than hire software engineers, the actors used Claude Code to write the guidance, navigation and control software that keeps a vehicle steady and on course. They also organised the work as a team. Several Claude instances ran in parallel with assigned roles: one wrote code, one did research and a third reviewed what the first produced, with the humans acting as the leads, handing out tasks.</p><p>Anthropic's safeguards refused many of these requests, though not all, according to the report. The group worked around them by concealing what they were building and what the software was for, and by breaking the work into separate sessions so no single conversation exposed the whole picture.</p><p>They never got to a working weapon, as far as Anthropic could tell, but they <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/10/anthropic-report-biological-weapons-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/10/anthropic-report-biological-weapons-ai/">did test-fire a guided rocket</a>. It appears to have failed; within hours, they were back with Claude trying to diagnose what went wrong.</p><p>Anthropic found the activity through its own investigations into suspected weapons development, banned the accounts, and passed threat information to government and industry partners. However, the group had already built an offline simulation toolkit that runs without Claude or commercial tools like MATLAB, so some of its capabilities survived the ban.</p><p>The company's decision to release the report comes after several high-profile <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/08/31/new-ai-models-pose-risk-to-global-financial-system-bank-of-england-chief-warns/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/08/31/new-ai-models-pose-risk-to-global-financial-system-bank-of-england-chief-warns/">incidents involving AI models</a> raised concerns over whether they were becoming too powerful and posed a risk to public safety.</p><p>Over the past decade, the Houthis have slowly transformed from a local armed faction into a wider force, threatening neighbouring countries and attacking shipping lanes while consolidating control over Sanaa and north-west Yemen.</p><p>The Houthis have an estimated troop strength, according to the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/us/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/us/">US</a>-based Middle East Institute, of 350,000, including child soldiers, forced-conscript African refugees and foreign mercenaries.</p><p>The rebels rely primarily on cross-border smuggling networks and complex maritime supply routes that extend across the Arabian Sea, the Gulf of Aden and the Red Sea to secure a significant portion of their military and logistical needs, including weapons, drone components, ballistic missiles, and technology and equipment related to naval capabilities.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DFBZ5K4VQE5HL6VQMDJQE3K2ZY.jpg?auth=9938730eb199d0ccef83712f8d5169ebe4884fca0ab3731b3af56768499bdaf0&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5472&amp;height=3163" type="image/jpeg" height="3163" width="5472"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A mock Houthi-made drone at a square in Sanaa. EPA]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">YAHYA ARHAB</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Gaza documentary Naza receives record-breaking 25-minute ovation at Venice Film Festival]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/09/11/gaza-documentary-naza-receives-record-breaking-25-minute-ovation-at-venice/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/09/11/gaza-documentary-naza-receives-record-breaking-25-minute-ovation-at-venice/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Evelyn Lau]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 05:11:31 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Israeli filmmakers <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/08/24/no-other-land-directors-documentary-on-israels-calculated-mass-killing-to-premiere-at-venice/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/08/24/no-other-land-directors-documentary-on-israels-calculated-mass-killing-to-premiere-at-venice/">Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor</a> received a record-breaking standing ovation at the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/venice-film-festival/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/venice-film-festival/">Venice Film Festival</a> for <i>Naza</i>, their new documentary examining how Israel has selected targets for air strikes in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/gaza/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/gaza/">Gaza</a>.</p><p>The film received an ovation lasting about 24 and a half minutes following its premiere on Thursday, reportedly the longest in the festival's history. It surpasses the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2025/09/04/voice-of-hind-rajab-venice-standing-ovation-record/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2025/09/04/voice-of-hind-rajab-venice-standing-ovation-record/">23-minute, 50-second reception</a> given to Tunisian director Kaouther Ben Hania's Gaza-set drama <i>The Voice of Hind Rajab</i> at Venice last year.</p><p>Abraham and Szor, who are among the four directors of the Oscar-winning documentary <i>No Other Land</i>, appeared emotional during the applause, while members of the audience held up Palestinian flags.</p><p>Jonathan Glazer, director of the Oscar-winning <i>The Zone of Interest</i> and an executive producer on <i>Naza</i>, was also at the premiere and embraced the filmmakers following the screening.</p><p>The 80-minute documentary is the result of an investigation spanning almost three years, and features interviews with 24 Israeli military intelligence officers and soldiers about the systems used to identify targets in Gaza.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/EBSQNJ2ZJJCOXIJMHA7FRRMPFU.jpg?auth=a6bdc788e9f83a5c0ef50f7d8d500fd1da27940a68cfa7306f4269dd5dbc66b7&smart=true&width=1900&height=1002" alt="Israeli military and intelligence sources were filmed in secrecy on Tel Aviv rooftops, with their identities digitally concealed. Photo: Naza" height="1002" width="1900"/><p>The interviews were conducted in secrecy, with participants filmed at night on rooftops in Tel Aviv, and their faces and voices digitally altered to conceal their identities.</p><p>“We are letting the soldiers and the officers speak about what they did,” Abraham told AFP ahead of the premiere. “The goal was very simple: we wanted to hear, in as much detail as possible, how the systems of killing in Gaza looked, how they operated.”</p><h2><b>What does Naza mean?</b></h2><p>The film takes its title from a term used by Israeli intelligence to refer to collateral damage, indicating the number of civilians expected to be killed in an air strike.</p><p>Among the systems examined in the documentary is an AI-assisted programme known as Lavender, which has reportedly been used to identify potential targets using information gathered through surveillance of Gaza's telecommunications network.</p><p>Interviewees in the film describe how technology allowed targets to be identified and attacked on a large scale.</p><p>“It was done on a mass scale, like a factory,” one interviewee says.</p><p>Another describes the remote nature of the process as being “like a video game”.</p><p>The filmmakers say the testimony also examines the destruction of civilian homes, and attitudes within sections of the Israeli military and intelligence services towards Palestinian civilians.</p><p>Abraham said the filmmakers were able to gain access to military and intelligence sources because they are Israeli.</p><p>“As Israelis, we found ourselves wanting to use the kind of position that we have – the access that we have to people in our own society who went into the military – to look at that system,” he told <i>Variety</i>.</p><h2><b>Palestine at the Venice Film Festival</b></h2><p>Abraham and Szor previously worked together on <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/08/24/no-other-land-directors-documentary-on-israels-calculated-mass-killing-to-premiere-at-venice/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/08/24/no-other-land-directors-documentary-on-israels-calculated-mass-killing-to-premiere-at-venice/"><i>No Other Land</i></a>, alongside <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/09/16/no-other-land-oscar-palestine-basel-adra-hamdan-ballal/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/09/16/no-other-land-oscar-palestine-basel-adra-hamdan-ballal/">Palestinian filmmakers Basel Adra and Hamdan Ballal</a>.</p><p>The documentary, which examines the displacement of Palestinian communities in Masafer Yatta in the occupied West Bank, won the Academy Award for Best Documentary Feature in 2025.</p><p>The reception for <i>Naza</i> continues a run of prominent films about Gaza and Palestine at Venice.</p><p>Last year, <i>The Voice of Hind Rajab</i> was lauded for its portrayal of the final hours of five-year-old Palestinian girl Hind Rajab, who was killed in Gaza in January 2024.</p><p>Ben Hania is serving on this year's Venice jury, led by American actress and filmmaker Maggie Gyllenhaal, which will announce the festival's award winners on Saturday.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/Q3COPB6JMWQBNJX34BII5IR2AQ.jpg?auth=f07d3844c1a7b0f80f4f3c7459f7f72b2dc9148cd94f30b411a5ddc40248c78d&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2444&amp;height=1691" type="image/jpeg" height="1691" width="2444"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Israeli filmmakers Rachel Szor and Yuval Abraham, who have also co-directed No Other Land, at the 2026 Venice Film Festival. EPA]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">ETTORE FERRARI</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[New Abu Dhabi paid parking signs relate to future update, authority says]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/new-abu-dhabi-paid-parking-signs-relate-to-future-update-authority-says/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/new-abu-dhabi-paid-parking-signs-relate-to-future-update-authority-says/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Ajanta Paul]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 04:55:03 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Abu Dhabi's public parking operator said new paid parking signs installed in some areas of the emirate relate to a “future update”.</p><p>Q Mobility, which manages public parking and the capital's Darb road toll network, told <i>The National </i>it will notify customers when changes come into effect.</p><p>The appearance of the parking signs – featuring individual area codes – has prompted debate online over the prospect of new fees being introduced across different neighbourhoods.</p><p>Darb app users have also noticed an update on the parking and toll payment platform that now shows designated parking zones for different areas of the city.</p><p>“Regarding the new signs showing zoning information, there is currently no action required from customers at this time. They can continue using the existing payment methods and following the current parking regulations as usual,” a Q Mobility spokesperson told <i>The National.</i></p><p>“The information displayed on these signs relates to a future update, which has not yet affected the current parking process or services.”</p><p>The company advised the public to follow its official social media channels for updates.</p><h2><b>How Abu Dhabi parking works</b></h2><p>Under the Mawaqif system, motorists pay parking charges based on bay category, with spaces divided into standard and premium zones that have different rates and time limits, regardless of neighbourhood.</p><p>Standard parking, marked in black and turquoise, costs Dh2 per hour with a maximum stay of 24 hours per session. Premium parking, marked in white and turquoise, costs Dh3 per hour with a four-hour limit.</p><p>If a vehicle is moved to a different neighbourhood or sector within Abu Dhabi, the parking ticket remains valid until expiry, provided the motorist follows parking and colour-type rules.</p><p>If Q Mobility introduces a zoning system, the pricing structure may shift from a flat, bay-type model to one where location determines rates, similar to Dubai.</p><h2><b>Dubai parking rules might offer clue</b></h2><p>In Dubai, parking zones are labelled A through K, with each zone displayed on signs along with payment details. The zone determines not only the location, but also parking charges, maximum stay duration and payment methods.</p><p>Parking in prime commercial areas such as Downtown Dubai and Dubai Marina is typically more expensive, while residential areas tend to have lower rates.</p><p>Public parking is largely managed by Parkin, set up by the Dubai government in 2024 and operates 268,300 paid parking spaces across Dubai.</p><h2><b>Streamlining parking in Abu Dhabi</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/QAHGT275KVGC5LE2HWQSLRVM3Q.jpg?auth=5180f81a92defec619d75e9403670512a0e21d98324ffe7c9a027c936325a903&smart=true&width=1079&height=697" alt="A parking payment machine in Abu Dhabi. Photo: 
ITC" height="697" width="1079"/><p>The new zone system is part of a broader, phased initiative by Q Mobility to expand and digitise parking across Abu Dhabi. Since assuming Mawaqif operations in 2024 under Abu Dhabi Mobility and the Department of Municipalities and Transport, the company has introduced paid parking in stages across multiple districts.</p><p>Mussaffah has implemented paid parking in three phases since January 2026, covering sectors M1 to M24, and adding nearly 19,000 regulated spaces.</p><p>In Mohamed Bin Zayed City, Q Mobility introduced paid parking across sectors ME9 to ME12 and surrounding villa zones beginning in late 2025.</p><p>Al Shahama offers approximately 3,700 paid parking spaces, including bays designated for people of determination.</p><p>The AI-powered Free Flow paid parking system operates in select multi-storey and commercial parking facilities across Abu Dhabi Island and Mohamed Bin Zayed City, deducting fees directly from the Darb wallet.</p><p>Q Mobility has signed an agreement with parking operator Parkin to explore integrating its digital parking and toll platforms across the emirate.</p><p>When introduced, zoning-based parking will align with the reorganisation of the parking system that has been ongoing for the past year.</p><p>For now, Q Mobility has said there will be no changes to payment tariffs, methods or durations until the new system is rolled out. An official announcement will provide details on the zone map, payment methods and transition period.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/ZFFY6LJOCFCYBATTLH44Z5TDAY.jpeg?auth=2e30ccef327df5cba0f7b4dfba2e75fa30b379b21d85c32f9103273fd0afb395&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2000&amp;height=1500" type="image/jpeg" height="1500" width="2000"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A new paid parking sign in Corniche Street, Abu Dhabi.

condition]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/emirati-patient-grateful-for-support-after-20-years-of-treatment-in-germany-for-spinal-condition/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/11/emirati-patient-grateful-for-support-after-20-years-of-treatment-in-germany-for-spinal-condition/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National, Shireena Al Nowais]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 04:52:14 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>An Emirati woman who has received 20 years of advanced treatment in Germany for a debilitating spinal condition has told of her gratitude for the UAE-funded support.</p><p>Nouf Al Naqbi, 32, has travelled regularly from the Emirates to Germany to undergo a series of complex medical procedures to improve her mobility after developing severe scoliosis – which causes the spine to curve – as a child.</p><p>She is one of 117 Emirati patients receiving critical care in Germany, further highlighting the strength and significance of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/10/president-sheikh-mohamed-meets-german-president-in-berlin/">bilateral ties</a> with the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/uae-president-visit-uae-german-ties/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/uae-president-visit-uae-german-ties/">European country</a>.</p><p>Their care in Germany is covered by several UAE government bodies, including Abu Dhabi's Department of Health.</p><p>While thankful for the assistance, Ms Al Naqbi said she has reached the limit of what she can endure, with her journey from childhood to adulthood marked by operating theatres and doctors' rooms.</p><p>“I’m so tired,” she said. “I’m tired of all the treatment and the pain. </p><p>"I’m grateful to [President] Sheikh Mohamed and all the support I’ve received but I am tired,” she said. “I don’t want any more surgery.”</p><h2><b>Vital helping hand</b></h2><p>Her father, Ali Al Naqbi, said the family would never forget the support they received throughout her treatment.</p><p>“We are deeply grateful to President Sheikh Mohamed and the UAE government for supporting Nouf and covering her treatment over so many years,” he said.</p><p>“From checking on us to arranging appointments, they ensured that her treatment and care went smoothly. They have always stood beside us and we will never forget that.”</p><p>He is eager for his daughter to continue to undergo treatment in Germany and urged her to stay strong on the road to a better quality to life.</p><p>Mr Al Naqbi said there had been no indication when his daughter was born that she would face such serious medical problems. “She was born naturally at Al Wasl Hospital in Dubai,” he said. “There was nothing wrong with her at birth, thank God.”</p><p>Her parents were related but the family said doctors had not identified a specific genetic condition. One of her brothers later developed a similar curvature of the spine and underwent surgery in Dusseldorf, Germany in 2023.</p><p>Ms Al Naqbi was diagnosed with early-onset scoliosis at the age of eight. When she was about 11, the family brought her to Germany for treatment. She underwent thoracolumbar fusion surgery at the University Hospital of Cologne in 2006.</p><p>“We were initially offered treatment in America but we chose Germany because it was closer,” Mr Al Naqbi said. “Germany was also well known for this type of treatment, particularly spinal surgery.”</p><p>The procedure involved fixing part of her spine by using screws and hooks. “The doctor told us the operation had been successful,” her father said. “But as she grew older, the curvature began to worsen again.”</p><p>Ms Al Naqbi underwent several operations on her legs, including procedures to release contractures, which occur when muscles or other tissues tighten and restrict movement. Her most recent leg operation was in 2023.</p><p>Physiotherapy has become a constant part of her life. “We continued returning to Germany for her check-ups and physiotherapy,” Mr Al Naqbi said. “We would come almost every year and sometimes stay for about two months.</p><p>“She has spent her whole life having physiotherapy. We never really stopped.”</p><p>Despite years of treatment, Ms Al Naqbi cannot walk long distances, becomes tired easily and suffers from lower and middle back pain, as well as weakness, numbness, and pins and needles.</p><p>“Her nerves are always tight and her body is extremely sensitive,” her father said. “Even touching her hand or back can affect her.”</p><p>Last June, Ms Al Naqbi returned to Germany for another major spinal operation. It became the latest chapter in a medical journey that has shaped her childhood and her adult life.</p><p>Asked how the family coped with spending so much time away from home, Mr Al Naqbi said: “We had no choice. We were accompanying her for treatment.”</p><h2><b>Healthcare partnership</b></h2><p>For decades, Emiratis with complex conditions have travelled to German hospitals for specialist care, often remaining for long periods with relatives. </p><p>Those connections rarely receive the same attention as trade, energy or investment agreements, but enduring links have been built between Emirati families and German doctors, nurses and therapists.</p><p>For Ms Al Naqbi, the relationship between the countries is measured in years of treatment and the support that enabled her to receive it.</p><p>She dreams of a life without hospitals, operations and constant physiotherapy. She also hopes that one day she will meet President Sheikh Mohamed and thank him personally for the support she has received.</p><p>But after repeated procedures and years of pain, she says she does not want to undergo another operation.</p><p>“I am tired,” she said.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/ZSDSGEAALRBUBHMDU2F7BPVWD4.jpg?auth=0414af5f687e4fe7a8b8a60260a24db370bc76774f1f977e477dcc3964eafa5b&amp;smart=true&amp;width=787&amp;height=443" type="image/jpeg" height="443" width="787"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Nouf Al Naqbi is exhausted after receiving years of crucial treatment in Germany.
