Photo: Pepe Cucina" height="5840" width="4672"/><p>JBR has a new neighbourhood Italian, with Pepe Cucina bringing wood-fired dishes, handmade pasta and a lively atmosphere to the waterfront. A Saturday brunch is already in full swing, while a separate games bar offers foosball, darts and pool, and a dedicated children’s area keeps the little ones entertained</p><p><i>Address Beach Resort, Dubai</i></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/W5EXIPHQRJH55PZO3ORFOKYOHM.jpg?auth=6699424f81b6bea97ef7aa02a497897ae42c3d2893fd2fd828da36a8cf556110&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4724&amp;height=3149" type="image/jpeg" height="3149" width="4724"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Two-Michelin-starred Indian restaurant Gymkhana has made its way from London to Dubai. Photo: Gymkhana]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Alex Jeffries Photography Group</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[The Middle East is trapped in a post-9/11 world, 25 years on]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/11/911-25-anniversary-us-middle-east-iran-iraq-afghanistan-israel/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/11/911-25-anniversary-us-middle-east-iran-iraq-afghanistan-israel/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Paul Salem]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 04:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>As the world marks the 25th anniversary of the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/911-25th-anniversary-new-york/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/10/911-25th-anniversary-new-york/">attacks of September 11, 2001</a>, the Middle East is, in many ways, living in the long shadow of those events.</p><p>The 9/11 attacks showed the global reach of armed non-state actors. The war on terror badly damaged Al Qaeda and eventually helped destroy ISIS aspirations for a so-called caliphate, but it <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2024/07/18/isis-iraq-mosul/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2024/07/18/isis-iraq-mosul/">did not eliminate</a> the phenomenon. Instead, extremist organisations metastasised in Iraq, Yemen, Syria, Libya and beyond, while regional states, especially Iran, persisted with their own strategy of strengthening networks of such actors. Hezbollah has recently been dealt a strong blow, but groups like it continue to <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/03/03/few-in-lebanon-will-regret-hezbollahs-demise-after-being-dragged-into-another-war-with-israel/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/03/03/few-in-lebanon-will-regret-hezbollahs-demise-after-being-dragged-into-another-war-with-israel/">destabilise the region</a>.</p><p>The 9/11 attacks also showed that while superpowers remain immensely powerful, that power neither makes them safe from attack at home nor guarantees that they can prevail against demonstrably weaker adversaries.</p><p>The attack on Pearl Harbour in 1941 was undertaken by the Imperial Japanese Navy; the attacks of 9/11 were perpetrated by 19 hijackers <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2024/08/21/aviation-air-travel-airport-japan-security-safety/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2024/08/21/aviation-air-travel-airport-japan-security-safety/">armed largely with box cutters</a>. Russia today is unable to protect its homeland from attacks by a smaller and weaker Ukraine, or to impose its will on it. The US and Israel decapitated Iran’s leadership and have dominance over its skies, but they have not been able to protect American bases or American allies in the region, nor bend Tehran to their will.</p><p>The dynamics of asymmetric warfare, unconventional fighting forces and the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/">never-ending race</a> between offensive and defensive weapons have scrambled the old neat hierarchy of international power.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DWMZZEERWNHUDCEE3K5VRA3YR4?auth=4c7c4b523b1942d668220705821bc5909eaddf6703ab420671f4dfaf4e09d056&smart=true&width=2601&height=1463" alt="Hijacked United Airlines Flight 175 from Boston crashes into the South Tower of the World Trade Centre at 9.03 am in New York City on September 11, 2001. AFP" height="1463" width="2601"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/OTW7XH3CRN2WBEKZMDHXWMXVKE.jpg?auth=4185ad0ed019ee0748a58bfa4a2179533f9ac9152e9e52506e0bf4617664e9e7&smart=true&width=3600&height=2400" alt="A man calling out asking if anyone alive trapped amid the rubble needs help after the collapse of the first tower on 9/11. AFP" height="2400" width="3600"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/NMAK6S4HTRDIJDCGSWXDFJD7TM?auth=e55ff3b1290be0b026f6fe9948dfb91634e03f4446b0a1d85fdf70a8582fb686&smart=true&width=1468&height=826" alt="Marcy Borders, known as the 9/11 Dust Lady, seeks refuge in an office building after the twin towers collapsed. AFP" height="826" width="1468"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/UZ2IN4L7XRFG3AKFH4KZNMUOE4?auth=726975a946d48f07d2decaec9ad74e4d87ff53dcfe047c1f02cbde3be92eae58&smart=true&width=1535&height=863" alt="Former US president George W Bush with firefighter Bob Beckwith and other rescue workers on September 14, 2001, at the site of the destroyed World Trade Centre. AFP" height="863" width="1535"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/M7EXQSEZNJATBDSOYPCDWIPF34?auth=ca02d7de9d676f0c07345253503f3906376b00d93ae260301a572ebda032d64c&smart=true&width=1312&height=738" alt="Businessman Edward Fine, who survived the attack after walking down 87 floors of the WTC North Tower, is covered in ash as he walks through the debris. AFP" height="738" width="1312"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CTFGQNANNCKVHEYQZDLZTKU5VA.jpg?auth=76a57e47c6a9f637d8439623e59917a8eda1968cd4d47f56bd928a1ba75cdcc1&smart=true&width=2048&height=1367" alt="Smoke billows from the twin towers of the World Trade Centre after the hijacked planes crashed into them. AFP" height="1367" width="2048"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/EVKWILZ6AYS6HLJMVA7SRKFYKY.jpg?auth=d319420f123486be084da6088934fef4ca5b06a18f0f3152f5b51a08eb462816&smart=true&width=9075&height=5546" alt="The collapse of the twin towers led to toxic dust, asbestos and glass fibres in the air, while underground fires smouldered at Ground Zero until December 2001. Reuters" height="5546" width="9075"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/6IJVCJ4V2RSJIIOFJ5DOKMXO6U.jpg?auth=80ecadf952634babd9ef0aae110d2b47374a5899aa450f616f7845adf8da8279&smart=true&width=2048&height=1510" alt="Debris covers streets including Park Row, which is nearly half a kilometre away from Ground Zero, after the towers collapsed. AFP" height="1510" width="2048"/><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IPID6D35JWZLBS5OCEM43UUMYA.jpg?auth=6fe5282fe96d859c32285ee42964c809df8724550f22632b9b187c0b9e2db89d&smart=true&width=4123&height=3395" alt="Workmen begin dismantling the destroyed remains of the World Trade Centre on September 17, 2001. EPA" height="3395" width="4123"/><p>The US response to the 9/11 attacks demonstrated the peril of deciding to go to war based on “rage first, reason later”. The American wars in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2023/03/17/the-us-invasion-of-iraq-changed-the-world-but-not-in-the-way-it-was-meant-to/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2023/03/17/the-us-invasion-of-iraq-changed-the-world-but-not-in-the-way-it-was-meant-to/">Afghanistan and Iraq</a> certainly had some strategic thinking and goals behind them, but the Afghanistan conflict in particular was driven in large part by the George W Bush administration’s need, after being humiliated on 9/11 both politically and strategically, to react in a rapid and overwhelming manner.</p><p>There was much thinking about how the first weeks of both wars would unfold, and indeed they produced quick and impressive military victories, but far less thinking about what the post-war political and security end state would be. Both conflicts ultimately proved to be strategic failures, leaving hundreds of thousands of Afghans and Iraqis dead, draining US lives and resources, empowering Iran in Iraq, and ceding Afghanistan <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/biden-gives-afghanistan-its-own-9-11-1.1203806" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/biden-gives-afghanistan-its-own-9-11-1.1203806">back to the Taliban</a>.</p><p>We have been reliving this cycle over the past three years, after Israel suffered what it considered its own 9/11 – the Hamas-led attacks of October 7, 2023. Then-US president Joe Biden <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/10/30/us-biden-israel-gaza-netanyahu/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2023/10/30/us-biden-israel-gaza-netanyahu/">warned</a> Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu not to repeat America’s mistake of deciding policy based on rage rather than reason, but the Israeli leadership used rage to <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/06/15/us-iran-israel-lebanon-hezbollah-forever-front-conflict-strait-of-hormuz-framework/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/06/15/us-iran-israel-lebanon-hezbollah-forever-front-conflict-strait-of-hormuz-framework/">launch wars</a> on “seven fronts”. And while these wars have swept away the old fragile order in the region, so far they have led to a condition of perpetual conflicts rather than to a new, politically thought-through order.</p><p>In a more straightforward geopolitical sense, the ongoing Iran war is partly the result of the previous American conflicts in Afghanistan and Iraq.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CM4ZVRZLBRBRVA4W6G3ACZXRYU.jpg?auth=fd417ec43af651b32ff7f02e23dc8c72e5655b381e3976a6f1e26f61e8b446a7&smart=true&width=3000&height=2291" alt="British and American forces fire artillery rounds during a live fire exercise at Bagram Airbase in Afghanistan in June 2002. Getty Images" height="2291" width="3000"/><p>Before 9/11, Tehran had been somewhat contained – hemmed in by Saddam Hussein’s Iraq to its west and the Taliban regime to its east. The US’s post-9/11 wars <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/mena/fall-of-saddam-reshaped-the-middle-east-1.719782" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/mena/fall-of-saddam-reshaped-the-middle-east-1.719782">removed both of them</a>. It is true that the Bush administration figured Baghdad and Kabul would be conquered first, and that Washington could then turn its firepower on Iran and its ally Syria. But the wars played out very differently, particularly in Iraq. The US got bogged down in both wars, and far from it being weakened, Tehran emerged as the main strategic victor in Iraq, from where it could <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/06/27/iran-israel-war-middle-east/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/06/27/iran-israel-war-middle-east/">spread its influence</a> more decisively into Syria, Lebanon and soon Yemen as well.</p><blockquote><p>The sooner Iran, Israel and the US can put reason first – and rage second – the sooner the region can find a stable outcome to this latest round of wars</p></blockquote><p>The US launched its post-9/11 wars to remake the Middle East in its image, much as it had remade Germany and Japan after the Second World War; instead, these wars ended up remaking the Middle East, to a significant degree, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/12/17/is-the-era-of-militias-in-the-arab-world-finally-ending-iraq-may-have-just-said-so/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/12/17/is-the-era-of-militias-in-the-arab-world-finally-ending-iraq-may-have-just-said-so/">in Iran’s image</a>, with Iranian proxies dominating four Arab capitals as well as projecting power into Palestine. In that sense, this latest war is partly an attempt to undo the strategic damage done by the previous two.</p><p>The 9/11 attacks were intended by their perpetrators to show that the US was a paper tiger, and to launch a series of confrontations that would chase it out of the Middle East. Indeed, the terrible logic of 9/11 was partly built on events two decades earlier, when Iran-backed militants, in co-ordination with the emerging Hezbollah, carried out devastating attacks on the US embassy and Marine barracks in Beirut. In response, the Ronald Reagan administration basically <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/beirut-suicide-bomb-reminds-us-of-the-folly-of-intervention-1.598948" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/beirut-suicide-bomb-reminds-us-of-the-folly-of-intervention-1.598948">cut and ran</a>.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/siBadA1MdIkMLY3XXU86STGb0zw=/cloudfront-eu-central-1.images.arcpublishing.com/thenational/FD75XWUX5FHFVHYPYO5AL72OZM.JPG" alt="January 29, 2002: US President George Bush identifies Iraq, Iran and North Korea as part of an 'axis of evil' in his State of the Union address. In December, he declares full victory over ISIS. AFP" height="1186" width="1920"/><p>The lesson drawn by Iran and armed non-state actors like Hezbollah was that America was indeed a paper tiger and that terrorism could work: inflict enough pain, and it would retreat. The <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2024/10/18/yahya-sinwar-led-palestinians-down-a-dark-road/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2024/10/18/yahya-sinwar-led-palestinians-down-a-dark-road/">attacks of October 7</a> were partly a playing out of that logic.</p><p>The responses of the Bush administration and now the Donald Trump administration demonstrated that the US is not a paper tiger at all, and that it remains by far the world’s strongest military power and is willing to use that force. But while not a paper tiger, it has at times taken on the demeanour of an <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/31/trump-vows-response-to-latest-iran-attacks-on-us-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/31/trump-vows-response-to-latest-iran-attacks-on-us-bases/">enraged bull</a>, devastating opponents and causing widespread carnage, but without enough strategic forethought and with little to show for its rampages. Post-October 7, Israel has reacted in a similar way.</p><p>Regardless of their causes or the forethought behind them, major wars have major consequences. The post-9/11 wars changed the Middle East, but not markedly for the better, ultimately spreading the scourge of armed non-state actors and empowering Iran. The new round of wars since October 7, and particularly the <a href="http://thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/29/middle-east-war-iran-hormuz-red-sea-iraq-saudi-arabia-ukraine/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="http://thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/29/middle-east-war-iran-hormuz-red-sea-iraq-saudi-arabia-ukraine/">Iran war</a>, will also have major consequences.</p><p>In the Levant, Tehran is already markedly weaker, and in both Syria and Lebanon new states are moving to take advantage of this weakening to rebuild state sovereignty. In the Gulf, however, the wars – and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/01/middle-east-us-iran-israel-uae-lebanon-gulf/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/01/middle-east-us-iran-israel-uae-lebanon-gulf/">intermittent negotiations</a> – leave the fate of that region very much in the balance.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5ROLWFDYCBJ6F5OJ64DFXPWJXM.jpg?auth=c7f43399c7ea01b6e01267b33723f5710314f3c36658e1b6190d850e9f569b3f&smart=true&width=4840&height=3102" alt="A wall painting of late Iranian supreme leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei in Tehran on Wednesday. EPA" height="3102" width="4840"/><p>In the Palestine-Israel arena, the conflict has only been more deeply entrenched, with Gaza cataclysmically devastated and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/08/20/west-bank-palestine-israel-us-uk-zionists-arthur-balfour/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/08/20/west-bank-palestine-israel-us-uk-zionists-arthur-balfour/">Israeli settlers</a> on the West Bank escalating their attacks – but these rampages, without any long-term political vision, only deepen the conflict and bring no lasting security to either side.</p><p>One of the main lessons of 9/11 should be the Clausewitzian one: war must remain subordinate to political purpose, rather than allowing passion and the momentum of violence to determine strategy. Today, Mr Trump is eager to bring this latest war to an end, reach a <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/20/iran-us-middle-east/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/07/20/iran-us-middle-east/">reasonable resolution</a> for the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz and prevent Iran from acquiring a nuclear bomb – which Iran itself publicly insists it does not want.</p><p>But similar to the US and Israel – and maybe even more so – the Islamic Republic appears to be still largely <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/02/20/even-if-america-bombs-change-for-iran-is-destined-to-come-from-within/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/02/20/even-if-america-bombs-change-for-iran-is-destined-to-come-from-within/">driven by rage</a>, both ideological and nationalistic; and while many voices even within the regime seem to be clamouring for a reasonable path forward, the voices of rage are winning the day.</p><p>Rage over reason is not a strategy. The sooner countries such as Iran, Israel and the US can put reason, common interests and sustainable solutions first – and rage, trauma and grievances second – the sooner the region can find a stable outcome to this latest round of wars and a co-operative and prosperous way forward.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/XGLZOGMXTZGYPOUN3EGX5LBMZI.jpg?auth=c88744af564e0780b52ab497410fcb0e6b794b7d9bdcac8b69d4974f69e8b799&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2520&amp;height=1417" type="image/jpeg" height="1417" width="2520"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Reuters / AFP and The National]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu"></media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Premier League predictions: City beat United in Manchester derby thriller]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/09/11/premier-league-predictions-city-beat-united-in-manchester-derby-thriller/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/09/11/premier-league-predictions-city-beat-united-in-manchester-derby-thriller/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Gareth Cox]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:25:36 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<h2><b>Saturday: Aston Villa v Nottingham Forest (6pm)</b></h2><p>Villa avoided a third successive defeat by drawing 0-0 at Hull last weekend, but Unai Emery's side are still waiting for the first point, or even their first goal, of the domestic season. That form was carried over into the Champions League as Enzo Maresca's men <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2026/09/09/mbappe-and-courtois-key-as-real-madrid-survive-inter-scare-haaland-hits-double-for-man-city/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2026/09/09/mbappe-and-courtois-key-as-real-madrid-survive-inter-scare-haaland-hits-double-for-man-city/">defeated Porto 2-0</a> on Tuesday with both goals coming from Erling Haaland, who has already found the back of the net five times this season.</p><p><b>Prediction: Man United 2 Man City 4</b></p><h2><b>Monday: Leeds United v Newcastle United (11pm)</b></h2><p>Leeds have yet to taste defeat in the league after last week's draw at Brighton made it five points from three games. Getty Images]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Mike Hewitt</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Haaland or Mbappe? Who has the best chance of breaking Ronaldo’s Champions League goals record?]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/09/11/haaland-or-mbappe-who-has-the-best-chance-of-breaking-ronaldos-champions-league-goals-record/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/09/11/haaland-or-mbappe-who-has-the-best-chance-of-breaking-ronaldos-champions-league-goals-record/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Andy Lewis]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Erling Haaland’s brace against Porto on Tuesday night maintained the Manchester City striker’s incredible scoring rate in the Uefa Champions League.</p><p>Haaland now has <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2026/09/09/mbappe-and-courtois-key-as-real-madrid-survive-inter-scare-haaland-hits-double-for-man-city/">59 goals from 59 games</a> in European club football’s elite competition, giving him the best goals-per-game ratio of any notable player in its history.</p><p>In fact, he is the only player to have scored a significant number of goals at a rate of at least one per game. It’s also the best scoring rate of the three active players among the Champions League's all-time top scorers: Haaland, Harry Kane and Kylian Mbappe.</p><p>The benchmark, however, is still some way off for all three. <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/08/10/cristiano-ronaldos-race-to-1000-goals-when-will-al-nassr-star-hit-historic-milestone/">Cristiano Ronaldo</a> is out in front with a staggering 140 goals from 183 appearances in the competition.</p><p>So, this raises the question: Can one of this generation’s elite goalscorers actually beat Ronaldo’s record?</p><p><a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/world-cup-2026/2026/07/06/bellingham-and-kane-combine-as-10-man-england-eliminate-world-cup-co-hosts-mexico/">Kane</a> has an impressive haul of 54 goals in 70 games, but at 33, the England captain can almost certainly be ruled out of the race. Sadly for him, he stayed at Tottenham Hotspur for far too long. </p><p>That leaves us with Haaland and Mbappe. Below is an appraisal of each man’s chances of going down as European club football’s greatest-ever goalscorer.</p><h2><b>Erling Haaland</b></h2><p>Haaland, 26, made his Champions League debut for RB Salzburg against Genk on September 17, 2019, and scored a hat-trick. Of course he did.</p><p>Since then, as stated above, he has scored at a rate of a goal per game in the competition. His best return came in 2022/23, when he scored 12 times in 11 games and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2023/06/10/incredible-manchester-city-complete-treble-with-champions-league-win/">ended up with a winner’s medal</a>. He currently sits seventh on the all-time list.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/A45KND7OQ5EAVJGW3KBB2LFDTA.png?auth=8232528050981399010113331b7399e5a15ad449d2d8f0853215ebf37c0d2a76&smart=true&width=1080&height=1248" alt="" height="1248" width="1080"/><p>This season will be his eighth in the Champions League. Among those, there is an outlier – 2021/22 – when he played just three games, scoring as many goals, as his then club, Borussia Dortmund, made an early exit.</p><p>Every other season, he has played at least eight games. If we remove that campaign and the current one, then he has a tally of 54 goals in 55 games across six seasons, at an average of nine goals per season.</p><p>By that calculation, he will need another 9.1 seasons (including the current one) to get the 82 goals he requires to break Ronaldo’s record – form, fitness and participation willing, of course.</p><p>That means Haaland needs to continue scoring at the same rate until the 2035/36 season. The Norwegian star, born in July 2000, will be 35 by then. It’s a big ask to be so consistent for so long, but in this era of supreme athleticism, it’s far from inconceivable. There’s also the frightening prospect that he could still get better and increase his goal output.</p><h2><b>Kylian Mbappe</b></h2><p>Mbappe, 27, has 71 goals in 99 Champions League games following his strike in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/2026/09/09/mbappe-and-courtois-key-as-real-madrid-survive-inter-scare-haaland-hits-double-for-man-city/">Real Madrid’s 2-1 victory over Inter Milan</a> in their opening game of the 2026/27 competition. That took him to joint-fifth with Raul in the all-time list. </p><p>He made his UCL debut on September 27, 2016, with a 13-minute cameo off the bench in Monaco’s 1-1 draw with Bayer Leverkusen.</p><p>He is now into his 11th season of Champions League football. Mbappe has managed at least eight games in each of his first 10 seasons, making his average a pleasingly concise seven goals per season, with 70 goals across the 10 completed campaigns.</p><p>At that rate, he will need exactly 10 more to chalk up the 70 goals he requires to surpass Ronaldo. Mbappe will be 38 heading into the back end of the 2036/37 season.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/NQYRR7OTAJEPBM36WA2CNQENXE.png?auth=f9bc3335558d839ed4a6eb0b134b67c00777da2cd3a37204b7ccd34cdd623429&smart=true&width=1080&height=640" alt="" height="640" width="1080"/><p>It's clear he needs to speed up, and there's ample evidence to suggest that he has. His goals-per-game rate has spiked sharply since he moved to Real Madrid in 2024.</p><p>The France striker managed an incredible 15 goals in 11 games last term – his best return – while he has a total of 22 in 25 games in his two completed seasons at the club.</p><p>At an average of 11 per campaign, he would need another six-and-a-half seasons to potentially put him within striking distance of the record by the 2032/33 season, when he will be 34, turning 35 in December. </p><h2><b>Verdict</b></h2><p>The numbers suggest that both Haaland and Mbappe stand a reasonable chance of surpassing Ronaldo’s mark if they can stay fit and play in the majority of matches for their respective clubs.</p><p>Both will be helped by the extra games offered by the competition’s new format, with teams now guaranteed eight league phase games as opposed to the six group games Ronaldo used to get before the knockouts. </p><p>If anything, this exercise really underlines Ronaldo’s incredible longevity and potency in front of goal. Interestingly, he scored goal number 140 on his 183rd and final Champions League appearance in a 2-0 win for Manchester United over Villarreal on November 23, 2021. He was 36 years, nine months and 18 days old at the time.</p><iframe width="560" height="315" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/3JQkveP0UL4?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen title="Cristiano Ronaldo&#39;s Top 20 Champions League goals"></iframe><p>Should Mbappe manage to stay at Madrid into the twilight of his career, then you would have to give him a shot, given the number of goals he has scored since his move to Los Blancos, and their consistency in reaching the latter stages of the competition year after year. Ronaldo was 33 when he left Madrid, while Mbappe's compatriot, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/sport/football/2026/02/03/karim-benzema-joins-al-hilal-in-stunning-deadline-day-move-as-cristiano-ronaldo-sits-out-al-nassr-match/">Karim Benzema</a>, stayed there until he was 35. </p><p>That said, averaging 11 per season to get there by the time he is 35 is a big ask, and he may need to keep playing into the second half of his 30s to eventually top the list. </p><p>Haaland, 19 months younger than Mbappe, will feel that 141 is an achievable target if he can regularly play 10 or more games per season in the competition. </p><p>As the younger man, and with the better goals-per-game ratio, you'd have to make the Manchester City striker a firm favourite. The X-factor is, of course, Mbappe's Real Madrid form. His 15 last season was the third most ever behind Ronaldo in 2015/16 (16) and 2013/14 (17). </p><p>Either way, it seems certain that both men will finish their careers considered among the finest goalscorers in modern football history. If either eventually stands alone as the best, remains to be seen.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/QFMBRIFA75A47II3PMBSS26YXA.jpg?auth=358a6ed979c19ce25c4a7403de33822598f14d6bee74537df82a6aff70769b42&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4800&amp;height=2700" type="image/jpeg" height="2700" width="4800"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Erling Haaland, left, and Kylian Mbappe were both on target in the Champions League this week. EPA/Getty Images]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Palestinian school year brings excitement for pupils and parents despite difficult conditions]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/palestinian-school-year-brings-excitement-for-pupils-and-parents-despite-difficult-conditions/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/11/palestinian-school-year-brings-excitement-for-pupils-and-parents-despite-difficult-conditions/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Nagham  Mohanna]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>At seven years old, Yemen Abu Odeh is excited about eventually starting school. He should have started classes last year and attended kindergarten before that, but the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/gaza" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/gaza">Gaza</a> war that erupted in October 2023 made that impossible. </p><p>Gaza’s educational infrastructure <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2024/09/04/gazas-children-question-their-future-amid-confusion-over-start-of-school-year/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2024/09/04/gazas-children-question-their-future-amid-confusion-over-start-of-school-year/">sustained extensive damage</a> during the war. More than 97 per cent of schools were damaged or destroyed, and others required major rehabilitation after being used as shelters for people displaced by the fighting. Hundreds of teachers and administrative staff were killed.</p><p>“During the previous years of the war there was no education, and schools and learning centres were not available as they are now,” said Iman Abu Odeh, Yemen's mother. </p><p>“Many children therefore lost educational opportunities and entire school years. Yemen missed both kindergarten and school. He was four when the war began.” </p><p>Children relied on online classes, private centres and tent classrooms to continue their education. Local charities, international aid organisations and community volunteers operated hundreds of temporary learning spaces, while some private schools continued to offer fee-based education.</p><p>However, there has been a push to restore educational facilities after a fragile ceasefire halted all-out fighting between Hamas and Israel last October, although <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/05/01/gaza-children-schools-education-human-rights-war-conflict-palestine-israel/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/05/01/gaza-children-schools-education-human-rights-war-conflict-palestine-israel/">conditions for schooling</a> in the devastated Palestinian territory remain far from ideal. </p><p>Gaza's new school year will start on September 19, according to Ahmad Al Najjar, director of public relations at the territory's Ministry of Education. </p><p>He said only 530,000 of the 541,000 school pupils could be provided with an education, explaining that “present conditions make it impossible to educate every student in Gaza”. </p><p>“More than 20,000 students and over 770 teachers and education employees have been killed since the war began. This has created a major challenge in finding enough teaching and administrative staff to operate the learning centres,” Mr Al Najjar told <i>The National</i>.</p><p>“Another challenge is that some parents are reluctant to send their children to school because of the war, economic hardship, limited public awareness and other problems created by the conflict.” </p><h2><b>'Everything is inadequate'</b></h2><p>Ms Abu Odeh's family was displaced from Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza to the central city of Deir Al Balah and her husband has been unemployed since the war began. </p><p>Besides Yemen, she has two older children of school-going age who attend classes for three hours a day at a nearby government school, where children receive instruction in five or six core subjects, depending on their level.</p><p>“There are better schools, but they charge registration fees and require families to pay for uniforms and supplies,” she said. “My children attend school, but we have not been able to provide them with stationery or school bags because we have no money.”</p><p>Ms Abu Odeh said she had noticed a clear decline in her children’s academic performance but saw no way to improve the situation.</p><p>“Schools today bear no resemblance to those that existed before the war, whether in terms of teachers, buildings, appearance, or services,” she said. “<a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/05/28/gaza-reconstruction-plans-fall-short-on-health-and-education/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/05/28/gaza-reconstruction-plans-fall-short-on-health-and-education/">Everything is inadequate</a>, offering children only the minimum educational benefit.”</p><p>Despite the difficulties and shortcomings, parents in Gaza, and their children, seemed determined not to give up on education.</p><p>“Families have generally sought to return their children to the classroom despite the systematic destruction inflicted on the education system,” Mahmoud Al Majdalawi, a social activist, told <i>The National</i>.</p><p>Children are eager to return to class despite all the difficulties they face, Mr Al Majdalawi said, citing the example of his 10-year-old nephew who has to walk two kilometres to reach school.</p><p>“That is a very long distance for a 10-year-old child, who must walk under the scorching sun or in the rain during the coming winter,” he said.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/2ML2WDBBCFA6XAH7NFSGWJREWI.JPG?auth=1d484bee32f9e729ae57d07d486e474094c5584297460650d60e1f373c28f479&smart=true&width=6000&height=4000" alt="Palestinian pupils cross an Israeli checkpoint near Hebron as they return to school for the new academic year in the Israeli-occupied West Bank. Reuters" height="4000" width="6000"/><h2><b>Learning under fear</b></h2><p>Palestinian pupils in the occupied <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/west-bank" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/west-bank">West Bank</a> have not been unaffected by the war in Gaza, which triggered an increase in military raids and settler violence in the Israeli-occupied territory. </p><p>“We were displaced from Tulkarm refugee camp more than a year ago because of Israeli military operations,” said Samer Al Jayousi, 42. “I have seven children, four of whom are school pupils aged between 10 and 17.</p><p>“Moving from one school to another because of displacement has affected my children’s academic performance. They also missed a considerable period of education as we moved from place to place.”</p><p>Mr Al Jayousi listed further displacement, children's safety and the rising cost of transport and school supplies as the greatest concerns for parents in the West Bank, where the new academic year began on September 6. </p><p>“Families with several children struggle greatly with school expenses but the fear for our children is even harder than the financial burden,” he said. “We are afraid of settlers who enter areas surrounding schools, as well as Israeli military operations that take place near schools from time to time.</p><p>“Last year, my son Sohaib was <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/08/30/inside-the-israeli-settler-tactics-driving-a-co-ordinated-campaign-to-reshape-the-west-bank/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/08/30/inside-the-israeli-settler-tactics-driving-a-co-ordinated-campaign-to-reshape-the-west-bank/">attacked by settlers</a> while he was on his way to school. He injured his hand and spent two days in hospital. The effects of the injury lasted for more than two months and affected his ability to write.”</p><p>According to the Palestinian Authority's Ministry of Education, 634,294 children are enrolled in 1,967 government schools in the West Bank, while another 163,787 pupils attend 480 private schools and 48,208 attend 90 schools operated by UNRWA, the UN agency for Palestinian refugees.</p><p>Out of 54,196 teachers working in Palestinian schools, 40,747 are in government schools, 11,578 in private schools and 1,871 in UNRWA schools.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/4WY5WWFWWVFCBFPZTC4TMWLSGE.JPG?auth=4bf08c481b219a6428238cc41d8433021322a4bd39b251a61116894c6194dad0&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5500&amp;height=3667" type="image/jpeg" height="3667" width="5500"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A Palestinian flag flies over a school building in Qusra, a village in the Israeli-occupied West Bank, as pupils return to school for the new academic year. It doesn't matter – schoolchildren should see them all]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/feedback/2026/09/11/mercator-or-equal-earth-maps-it-doesnt-matter-schoolchildren-should-see-them-all/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/feedback/2026/09/11/mercator-or-equal-earth-maps-it-doesnt-matter-schoolchildren-should-see-them-all/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><b>Write to us: </b><a href="https://form.typeform.com/to/mM1xOya8?typeform-source=www.thenationalnews.com" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://form.typeform.com/to/mM1xOya8?typeform-source=www.thenationalnews.com"><b>Share your feedback on the news of the week</b></a></p><p><b>Different maps for different folks</b></p><p>I write in reference to Adla Massoud’s article <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/04/mercator-map-un-equal-earth-us/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/04/mercator-map-un-equal-earth-us/"><i>UN votes to adopt new map showing Africa's true size despite US opposition</i></a> (September 4): as someone who makes maps for a living, I will say that the standard Mercator projection should not be used as the baseline map of the world in schools. This is because better maps and projections exist to show relative size. If you’re getting into projections for navigation or mapping purposes with your students, I think it’s important to show the many other projections that exist. It’s also important to talk about why it’s so difficult to make a round area display accurately on a flat rectangle. That’s an important educational point, along with correctly showing students what the world around them looks like. <i>– Caley Marissa</i></p><p>The Mercator projection isn’t perfect, but no projection of a rounded Earth is going to be. What the Mercator projection excels at is maintaining position and national borders accurately. Yes, it distorts size. The [Equal Earth] map projection also has distortions. <i>– Sebastien Sinclair Dalin, Des Moines, US</i></p><p>If I were a teacher, I would display multiple maps as well as have globes. Prior to the creation of the “Equal Earth” map, I would’ve said the “Robinson projection” would be most standard, which I still think is pretty good at minimising distortion, though it still sacrifices actual size near the poles. I’d also show pupils “Goode’s homolosine“ and the “Equidistant Conic projection“. I think it’s cool to learn about all the ways maps can be made and used to display space. It’s also important to express how certain things have to be sacrificed when displaying a sphere on a flat surface. You cannot have perfect shape, distance, size and angles – therefore sacrificing at least one. There are maps that can display the sacrifice of each of those things individually, which is a cool way to study maps. <i>– Simon Cameron Dayrit</i></p><p><b>Ceasefire now!</b></p><p>I write in reference to David Vujanovic’s article <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/gulf/2026/09/09/tanker-war-20-how-us-iran-ship-attacks-are-escalating/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/gulf/2026/09/09/tanker-war-20-how-us-iran-ship-attacks-are-escalating/"><i>Tanker war 2.0: How US-Iran ship attacks are escalating</i></a> (September 9): the recent escalation in the conflict between the US and Iran is certainly not a healthy sign for the Middle East. This exchange of attacks has created fear, anxiety and uncertainty among people across the region. The public has already suffered greatly from prolonged conflicts, loss of innocent lives, displacement and disruption to their normal lives. The latest escalation only adds to their worries and raises the danger of the conflict spreading further. A genuine and lasting ceasefire is urgently needed. Military action can only bring more suffering; it cannot provide a permanent solution to complex political and security issues. The US and Iran, with the support of responsible international mediators, should return to dialogue and find a peaceful and practical way forward. <i>– K Ragavan, Bengaluru, India</i></p><p><b>AI and future relations</b></p><p>I write in reference to Sarmad Khan’s article <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/07/beyond-50-years-ai-and-energy-investment-at-heart-of-uae-germany-economic-ties/?utm_term=Autofeed&amp;utm_medium=Social&amp;utm_source=Twitter#Echobox=1788761501" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2026/09/07/beyond-50-years-ai-and-energy-investment-at-heart-of-uae-germany-economic-ties/?utm_term=Autofeed&amp;utm_medium=Social&amp;utm_source=Twitter#Echobox=1788761501"><i>Beyond 50 years: AI and energy investment at heart of UAE-Germany economic ties</i></a> (September 7): while a lot of countries are still talking in circles about artificial intelligence and energy security, the UAE and Germany are just getting on with building actual partnerships. <i>– Aisha Rahman</i></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/VJ43OJ7VR5E2XLDAZT4L4RPUHQ.jpg?auth=5210cedbfd040b08ca261ef29b25828f369e5d0b2c1645af39d00a40110c72af&amp;smart=true&amp;width=7200&amp;height=4579" type="image/jpeg" height="4579" width="7200"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[The detailed political world map in Mercator projection. Getty Images]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[The UAE and Germany are showing how partnership can meet 21st-century challenges]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/09/11/uae-germany-visit-middle-east-europe-politics-diplomacy-economy/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/editorial/2026/09/11/uae-germany-visit-middle-east-europe-politics-diplomacy-economy/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National Editorial]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Separated by thousands of kilometres of desert, sea and mountains, Germany and the UAE might, at first glance, seem worlds apart. On the contrary, their relationship is one that goes back more than a century. A striking example of this closeness is the February 1904 <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/09/linked-by-literature-and-legacy-cultural-ties-between-uae-and-germany-go-back-to-zayed-the-great-and-beyond/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/09/linked-by-literature-and-legacy-cultural-ties-between-uae-and-germany-go-back-to-zayed-the-great-and-beyond/">photograph of Sheikh Zayed bin Khalifa Al Nahyan</a> seated at a majlis in Abu Dhabi. Taken by German traveller and photographer Hermann Burchardt, the image remains on display in a Berlin museum.</p><p>Over the decades, this moment of connection deepened as the Emirates and Germany moved forward. In more recent times, Germany, along with others, traditionally looked to the UAE for energy, and the Emirates proved a prominent and reliable partner in that relationship. However, as President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed’s <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/president-sheikh-mohamed-bin-zayed-arrives-in-germany-to-begin-state-visit/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/president-sheikh-mohamed-bin-zayed-arrives-in-germany-to-begin-state-visit/">state visit</a> to Europe’s largest economy gathers pace this week, the ties between the two countries have for some time reflected something much deeper.</p><p>Certainly, there are real people-to-people ties forged by more than 50 years of diplomatic relations. The links between Berlin and Abu Dhabi have borne fruit in education, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/09/why-scientists-in-germany-are-studying-the-variety-and-beauty-of-traditional-gulf-homes/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/09/why-scientists-in-germany-are-studying-the-variety-and-beauty-of-traditional-gulf-homes/">culture</a>, archaeology and research. German schools have <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/german-school-marks-50-years-of-building-bridges-in-abu-dhabi/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/09/german-school-marks-50-years-of-building-bridges-in-abu-dhabi/">educated generations of Emiratis</a>, while organisations such as the Goethe-Institut have helped deepen mutual understanding. Today, thousands of Germans live, work and thrive in the Emirates.</p><p>However, while an increasingly unpredictable and competitive world offers new opportunities, it also presents new challenges. Germany needs energy security, but it also stands to gain from investment, technology, resilient supply chains and new markets. At a time of major change across geopolitical and economic developments, the UAE and Germany offer each more than a source of energy. </p><p>The two countries already have a substantial economic relationship, with bilateral trade exceeding $15.5 billion last year and about 2,000 German companies operating in the Emirates. UAE investment is also helping Germany’s energy transition, including through Masdar’s 49 per cent stake in the 476-megawatt Baltic Eagle offshore wind farm. At the same time, Adnoc and Germany’s RWE are exploring further LNG opportunities. New agreements in the AI, energy and investment sectors stemming from <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2026/09/09/uae-president-arrives-in-germany-in-pictures/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2026/09/09/uae-president-arrives-in-germany-in-pictures/">Sheikh Mohamed’s visit</a> will deepen this co-operation. Germany is also the UAE's largest EU trading partner.</p><blockquote><p>The most intriguing part of this relationship may be what follows</p></blockquote><p>However, the most intriguing part of this relationship may be what follows. Germany possesses formidable industrial expertise, engineering and advanced technology. The UAE has capital, energy, infrastructure, logistics and access to some of the world’s fastest-growing markets. Co-operation in AI, manufacturing, renewable energy and infrastructure could become more significant than the traditional buyer-seller relationship that once characterised Europe’s ties to the Gulf.</p><p>There is also a diplomatic dimension. Germany is a major European power with influence in the continent and beyond. The UAE has built relationships across an increasingly interconnected world, including in Asia and Africa, while maintaining channels with countries that Europe may find difficult to engage. As conflicts continue to reshape the Middle East and Europe, such connectivity has strategic weight.</p><p>It has been a long journey from the day a German traveller took a snapshot of the political and social life of a then-unfamiliar Arab nation. Fast forward to 2026 however, and Sheikh Mohamed’s visit shows how that instance of connection eventually flourished into a modern, forward-thinking partnership.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/T75LPQKMRBA7VJWRC25CK3QN3Y.jpg?auth=ba38864151d44d722fbc295556150d199086ae7ae459ac785d5f353c20000dab&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4200&amp;height=2800" type="image/jpeg" height="2800" width="4200"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[President Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed’s state visit to Europe’s largest economy this week has highlighted the two nations' relationship. Abdulla Al Bedwawi / UAE Presidential Court]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Abdulla Al Bedwawi / UAE Presidential Court</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[VAT in the UAE: New October regulation will change how you manage compliance]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/2026/09/10/vat-in-the-uae-new-october-regulation-will-change-how-you-manage-compliance/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/2026/09/10/vat-in-the-uae-new-october-regulation-will-change-how-you-manage-compliance/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[David Daly]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Federal Tax Authority Decision No. 13 of 2026, effective from October 1, 2026, is about to comprehensively change how your business manages <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/07/28/from-vat-to-ai-uaes-push-to-shape-future-of-tax/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/07/28/from-vat-to-ai-uaes-push-to-shape-future-of-tax/">compliance for VAT</a>.</p><p>This will affect how supplier invoicing is managed and has <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2025/12/03/uae-vat-rule-changes-january-2026/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/economy/2025/12/03/uae-vat-rule-changes-january-2026/">specific financial implications</a> if you fail to correctly implement, control on an ongoing basis and respond when requested.</p><p>Let’s start with what will now be required to verify your supplier, of which there are two types.</p><p>Firstly, natural persons who are VAT registered. You may ask how that can happen. Imagine if the landlord of your office floor, where you pay rent of Dh500,000 ($136,120) a year, is an individual.</p><p>Because that person earns over the VAT registration threshold, they must register for the tax and levy it.</p><p>Before claiming the VAT charged, you now must meet that individual, in person or virtually, and have a copy of their identity proof, such as a passport or Emirates ID. Although it’s not mentioned, I’d imagine that they should be current at the time of the VAT input reclaim.</p><p>Secondly, when dealing with a licensed company, you must now verify that it is legally incorporated. A copy of their certificate of incorporation will suffice and as this never expires, it’s the best document to hold. Any other official database may be used, although none are listed by way of example.</p><p>This is then used to confirm a supplier’s name and address against the trade licence they’ve provided you. What’s worrying here is that it mentions employees and other related information.</p><p>The names mentioned on trade licences are the manager(s) in charge and occasionally some or all of the shareholders. Further, as these can change, you are at the mercy of your supplier updating this information in a timely manner to the relevant trade licence-issuing authority and forwarding the updated document to you. This will be an interesting exercise in larger organisations.</p><p>We’re not done. You must also verify the identity of the person representing the supplier, again with a passport or Emirates ID. The wording suggests that the individual must be empowered to represent the supplier. This I would read to mean is someone who can contract on their behalf. </p><p>That would narrow the pool of individuals considerably. Now you might also need confirmation of shareholding, notarised power of attorney or board resolutions.</p><blockquote><p>Another task required is to review and identify risks relating to each supplier</p></blockquote><p>For both types, you must verify that supplier’s place of business, either finding sufficient online proof or visiting the location. The latter would seem required in certain circumstances as you are also required to ensure that their premises are suitable for the purpose of delivering the goods or services you are purchasing from them.</p><h2><b>Identifying risks</b></h2><p>Another task required is to review and identify risks relating to each supplier. This challenge has three elements to overcome.</p><p>Should the supplier have changed offices more than twice in a 12-month period, or the same for the person your business interacts with, or the volume or nature of their business materially changes, it falls to you to clearly document and justify why this causes you no concern. You may be asked to present this information during an audit by a relevant authority.</p><p>You must also verify that each supplier, from whom you are planning to reclaim VAT, has a bank account where the value of annual purchases exceeds the VAT registration threshold or is expected to, over the subsequent 12 months.</p><p>This takes the form of getting a letter of confirmation from your supplier’s bank. The letter need not be addressed to you. </p><p>Scan the publicly available media to investigate whether your supplier is trading in a manner consistent with what you’ve been led to believe.</p><p>Where your supplier requests that payment is made to a non-UAE located bank, you must retain the evidence that there’s a reasonable commercial reason for the same.</p><p>Payment should never be made in cash unless there is a documented reason, commercially substantiable, for doing so. The legislation mentions thresholds, but I’m not aware of any bandwidths that detail these.</p><p>You must be content that the purchase price is a fair market price, as should the margin achieved. </p><p>You must also ensure that the supplier has the right, detailed on their trade licence, to sell the goods and services you are purchasing, and that they’ve title to them.</p><p>You must appoint someone internally to manage the above. Purchases less than Dh10,000 before VAT fall outside the above guidelines, unless in total, surpassing Dh100,000 in any 12-month period.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/YHLLRZD5WBC2TAL5KB73W6NIEY.jpg?auth=eab3f4a12bbc2645f896a73ed0d461b709d71cb406fde59aa98d1a5502764fa6&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5472&amp;height=3648" type="image/jpeg" height="3648" width="5472"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[New VAT rules require detailed information about suppliers. Getty Images ]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Ovidiu Dugulan</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Muslim Americans are still shaping their place in country, 25 years after 9/11]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/11/muslim-americans-are-still-shaping-their-place-in-america-25-years-after-911/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/11/muslim-americans-are-still-shaping-their-place-in-america-25-years-after-911/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Jihan  Abdalla]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 11 Sep 2026 03:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>One of Zaid Fouzi’s earliest childhood memories is from a family picnic in Ohio. He was four years old.</p><p>A man approached his mother, who wears a hijab, and said: “I’ll never forget <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/09/911-us-military-middle-east-wars/">9/11</a>. I’ll never forget what you people did.”</p><p>A verbal altercation with his father, laden with expletives, followed. Mr Fouzi and his older brother watched, stunned and frightened.</p><p>It was a year after the September 11 attacks of 2001, which killed nearly 3,000 people and had a profound and lasting impact on the US and the world — including on Muslim and Arab Americans.</p><p>Mr Fouzi, now 29 and a Syrian-American comedian, says that incident and others like it became life for Muslims growing up in the aftermath of 9/11.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/74ZLVIEFPVC6HC6VVX6GUCZGWA.jpg?auth=a57ed7189dc6c1d5e79a2e95803cd5a898971b74276b1725f08535d2f59c1dd5&smart=true&width=6643&height=4429" alt="Community members gather at the Islamic Centre of San Diego, California, on May 19, the day after a shooting there killed three people. The two teenage gunmen later shot themselves.  Middle East Images via AFP" height="4429" width="6643"/><p>“For me, growing up post-9/11 made me feel very protective of my identity,” he said. “I just always wore it like a badge of honour.”</p><p>A quarter of a century after the attacks, Muslim and Arab Americans say the discrimination and suspicion they endured have also instilled a determination to carve out their own place in American society.</p><p>To do so, they have challenged bias, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/11/muslims-used-as-scapegoats-as-islamophobic-political-rhetoric-rises-in-us/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/11/muslims-used-as-scapegoats-as-islamophobic-political-rhetoric-rises-in-us/">fought discrimination</a>, protested against surveillance and opposed accusations that they are not sufficiently American.</p><p>In the immediate aftermath of the attacks, FBI data showed a sharp rise in anti-Muslim hate crimes. The backlash also exposed the profoundly ignorant nature of prejudice: one of the first people killed in a post-9/11 hate crime was Balbir Singh Sodhi, a Sikh American who was shot outside his Arizona petrol station days after the attacks.</p><p>Muslim Americans who came of age after 9/11 have often found themselves having to educate others about their faith and defend their identities.</p><p>“You were essentially forced to be an ambassador to Islam, which forced you to learn more about it and be more articulate about what Islam says about a given issue,” said Youssef Chouhoud, an assistant professor at Christopher Newport University, who studies Muslim Americans.</p><p>He says that experience helped to produce a generation that is more conscious of its identity and more confident in asserting it — including through political participation.</p><p>Mr Chouhoud said <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2025/10/31/islamophobia-against-zohran-mamdani-tip-of-the-iceberg-as-more-politicians-spout-anti-muslim-rhetoric/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2025/10/31/islamophobia-against-zohran-mamdani-tip-of-the-iceberg-as-more-politicians-spout-anti-muslim-rhetoric/">Zohran Mamdani</a>, New York City’s first Muslim Mayor, and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/mena/arab-showcase/2025/08/29/abdul-el-sayed-michigan-us-senator-race/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/mena/arab-showcase/2025/08/29/abdul-el-sayed-michigan-us-senator-race/">Abdul El-Sayed</a>, the Democratic nominee from Michigan for the US Senate, were examples of a generation seeking greater political involvement and an expanded role in shaping American society.</p><p>Still, negative perceptions persist.</p><p>A Pew Research Centre survey conducted in May found that 43 per cent of US adults said Muslims are less patriotic than other Americans, while 42 per cent said Muslim Americans have a negative effect on the country.</p><p>Last month, far-right activist Jake Lang led a “Christian Crusade” demonstration in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/us-news/2024/02/12/palestinian-americans-in-dearborn-say-they-will-never-forget-bidens-stance-on-gaza/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/world/us-news/2024/02/12/palestinian-americans-in-dearborn-say-they-will-never-forget-bidens-stance-on-gaza/">Dearborn, Michigan</a>, home to the largest concentration of Arab Americans in the US.</p><p>Mr Lang, alongside hundreds of supporters, denounced what they called the “Great Replacement” of Christians and warned of the spread of “Sharia law”. He later ripped up pages of the Quran.</p><h2><b>Patriot Act</b></h2><p>After the attacks on the World Trade Centre in New York and the Pentagon in Virginia, the US government swiftly expanded its counter-terrorism and security apparatus.</p><p>Just 45 days after the attacks, Congress passed the Patriot Act, significantly increasing the government’s surveillance and law-enforcement powers in terrorism and national security investigations.</p><p>Although several key provisions expired in March 2020, civil liberties groups say the law’s legacy has endured, including its effect on Muslim Americans, who were subjected to disproportionate surveillance and scrutiny.</p><p>The effects, critics say, extend beyond the law itself.</p><p>Sumayyah Waheed, senior policy counsel at Muslim Advocates, a Washington-based civil rights organisation, says the Patriot Act and the creation of the Department of Homeland Security were measures that increased government power in the name of national security.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DH5RWYYFWVDPZFY7TVE4J4ZDFU.jpg?auth=b35e89efa5ba7aecfb8d3d506c5666423f75b7c5af17c0f198edda8f607628cf&smart=true&width=6048&height=4024" alt="Supporters gat
